Nissan Will Pay Toyota RAV4 Owners To Trade Their Car For A New Rogue Hybrid

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Nissan's very public struggles are well documented, so it shouldn't come as a surprise that the automaker is taking an aggressive stance with its newest and arguably most important model, the Rogue e-Power Hybrid compact crossover. Compact crossover sales account for a massive share of new car sales each year behind only the full-size pickup segment, with heavyweights like the Honda CR-V, Toyota RAV4, Tesla Model Y, and Nissan Rogue all making the list of the top 10 best-selling cars in the U.S. through Q3 of this year. The Rogue comes in at tenth place on that list, but it turns out Nissan isn't messing around when it comes to improving its ranking, as it's now offering a $1,500 trade-in bonus for anyone who trades a 2021-or-newer Toyota RAV4 for a new Rogue Hybrid.

Our friends at CarsDirect got wind of a dealer bulletin that Nissan sent to its dealers announcing the incentive, which was introduced on November 3 and will run through January 4, 2027.

In order to qualify for the $1,500 trade-in bonus, interested shoppers must have a 2021-or-newer Toyota RAV4 that they trade in when purchasing or leasing a 2027 Nissan Rogue Hybrid. The deal is transferable to members of the same household, and it can be stacked with Nissan's other current offers. Those other offers include a financing deal of 4.9% APR for 60 months, and a lease deal on the west coast where lessees pay $439 per month for 36 months with $4,959 due at signing and a mileage limit of 10,000 miles per year.

Jalopnik's own Andy Kalmowitz sampled the 2027 Nissan Rogue Hybrid at the first drive event in Santa Barbara, California, late last month which you can check out here, and he preferred driving the new Rogue Hybrid over the new Toyota RAV4 hybrid.

The 2027 Nissan Rogue Hybrid features a unique series hybrid drivetrain that operates primarily as an EV. The turbocharged 1.5-liter three-cylinder engine under the hood is solely responsible for charging the Rogue's 1.67-kWh battery, and is not connected to the wheels. The wheels are driven by electric motors mounted on the front and rear axles providing standard all-wheel-drive, and they're capable of scooting the Rogue from 0-to-60 in a quick 6.4 seconds without ever needing to be plugged in. It's rated for 41 mpg in the city, 36 mpg on the highway, and 38 mpg combined which is slightly worse than the RAV4 hybrid, but Andy says the Rogue's interior is a much more comfortable and luxurious space than the RAV4 provides.

SuperAger AI Connectome Predicts Alzheimer’s Years Early

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A neuroimaging and machine learning study introduces the Alzheimer’s Resilience-Vulnerability Connectome (ARC), a computational model that detects early functional brain changes linked to Alzheimer’s disease before clinical memory loss begins.

Developed by Kanhao Zhao and colleagues, the framework departs from standard paradigms by benchmarking patients with Alzheimer’s directly against “SuperAgers”, older adults who preserve youthful cognitive faculties past age 75. By defining the extremes of cognitive resilience and vulnerability through resting-state fMRI connectomes, ARC successfully stratifies asymptomatic older adults along this spectrum, accurately predicting accelerated cognitive decline, amyloid-β accumulation, and clinical Alzheimer’s conversion over 5 to 15 years.

Source: Neuroscience Research Consortium / Clinical Neuroimaging

Alzheimer’s disease (AD) is characterized by a decades-long silent incubation period. Pathological cascades involving amyloid-β aggregation, hyperphosphorylated tau tangle formation, neuroinflammation, and synaptic degradation unfold insidiously within the brain long before overt clinical memory loss, disorientation, or executive dysfunction emerges.

Identifying individuals during this pre-symptomatic window is critical for therapeutic interventions to halt irreversible neuronal death. However, traditional machine learning models and biomarkers designed to detect early pathology often compare Alzheimer’s patients to “cognitively normal” older adults. Because typical aging frequently harbors subclinical neuropathology and mild cognitive slowing, using average peers as a baseline muddies the analytical contrast, obscuring subtle network-level indicators of disease vulnerability.

To overcome this limitation, a research team led by Kanhao Zhao and colleagues re-anchored the biological baseline by investigating the opposite pole of cognitive aging: “SuperAgers”, individuals aged 75 and older whose memory and mental faculties match or exceed those of healthy individuals decades younger.

Publishing their findings, the investigators unveiled the Alzheimer’s Resilience-Vulnerability Connectome (ARC), a machine learning framework trained to map where an individual’s brain network resides on the spectrum between exceptional neurocognitive resilience and neurodegenerative collapse.

The fundamental innovation of the ARC framework lies in training algorithms on biological divergence rather than average health.

The researchers trained and calibrated the model using resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI) scans from two distinct cohorts, both aged 75 or older:

By isolating the whole-brain functional connectome, the coordinated synchrony among resting-state networks, including the default mode network (DMN), salience network, and executive control systems, ARC learned the distinct spatial and temporal connectivity patterns that separate extreme resistance from active neurodegeneration.

The model was subsequently validated in an independent replication cohort of 143 individuals, confirming that the connectome features captured generalizable neurobiological properties rather than cohort-specific noise.

Once the resilience-vulnerability axis was defined, the investigators deployed ARC to assess cognitively unimpaired individuals before symptoms appeared.

The model was applied to baseline rs-fMRI data from 818 cognitively intact older adults who were not classified as SuperAgers. Without knowledge of the participants’ clinical trajectories, ARC assigned each participant an objective score reflecting where their functional brain architecture fell along the SuperAger-to-Alzheimer’s continuum. The team replicated these continuous scoring metrics across an additional independent validation group of 497 individuals.

Longitudinal tracking over 5 to 15 years confirmed the prognostic power of this early connectomic profiling:

“These findings support the potential utility of [the tool] for early risk stratification among cognitively unimpaired older adults,” write Zhao and colleagues.

By establishing that functional connectome remodeling precedes macroscopic structural atrophy and clinical symptoms, ARC offers a non-invasive, radiation-free neuroimaging tool for patient stratification.

In clinical trials for disease-modifying therapies, such as monoclonal antibodies targeting amyloid or tau, enrolling participants when their connectomes first begin shifting away from resilient states could optimize drug efficacy before extensive synaptic loss occurs.

Mapping the specific functional hubs that preserve SuperAger cognition also provides fresh anatomical targets for neuromodulation, cognitive reserve enhancement, and preventive neurology.

How does the ARC framework differ from existing Alzheimer’s detection models?

Most diagnostic models compare Alzheimer’s patients to average older adults who may already harbor silent pathology. ARC instead compares Alzheimer’s patients directly to “SuperAgers”, older adults aged 75+ with exceptional, youthful memory retention, setting a clear biological continuum from extreme neurocognitive resilience to neurodegenerative vulnerability.

What imaging technology does ARC use to evaluate the brain?

The system analyzes resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI), tracking functional connectivity and spontaneous communication across distributed neural networks throughout the cerebrum without requiring invasive injections or radioactive tracers.

What did the longitudinal data reveal about people with AD-like connectomes?

Over follow-up windows spanning 5 to 15 years, cognitively unimpaired individuals whose resting-state brain connectomes tilted toward the Alzheimer’s profile showed faster rates of cognitive decline, accelerated accumulation of amyloid-β plaques, and a significantly higher likelihood of progressing to clinical Alzheimer’s disease.

A functional connectome identifies vulnerability to cognitive decline and Alzheimer’s disease progression

Identifying the neurobiological basis of cognitive vulnerability is essential for preventing Alzheimer’s disease (AD). We derived the Alzheimer’s Resilience-Vulnerability Connectome (ARC) by contrasting SuperAgers (SA), an extreme phenotype of preserved late-life memory, with AD using resting-state fMRI. ARC distinguished SA from AD in a discovery cohort (N = 290; AUC = 0.85) and replicated independently (N = 143), involving prefrontal, temporal, and insular networks and associating with brain age.

Applied to cognitively unimpaired adults (discovery: N = 818; replication: N = 497), ARC-based subtyping revealed SA-like and AD-like subgroups with comparable baseline cognition but divergent longitudinal trajectories. AD-like individuals exhibited faster cognitive decline, greater amyloid-β accumulation, and higher AD conversion risk. Exploratory GWAS nominated CLYBL and FRMD6 as candidate novel loci associated with the subtype difference.

These findings suggest that ARC captures a continuous connectomic dimension of cognitive aging spanning SA, cognitively typical aging, and AD, highlighting the potential of functional connectomic variation for early risk stratification in AD.

Tight Budget? This Familiar Destination Could Help You Cut Expenses.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

With less room in their budgets, people are turning to a familiar resource they may have overlooked.

Shoppers looking to cut costs are rediscovering an old favorite location in their neighborhoods: their local library.

According to a new report from NPR, consumers are returning to the library to get more without stretching their paychecks.

One avid reader, Izzy Rivera, picks up at least one book a week from local libraries near her residence in Topeka, Kansas. She said in an interview with NPR that it “just isn’t realistic” for her to regularly buy new books as she saves up for law school.

Rivera checks out both physical books and digital ones through the Libby library app. She isn’t ruling out buying books altogether. Last month, she went to her local library’s used book sale and bought 15 books for around $20.

There are other instances where the library provided Rivera with free or low-cost materials. For example, she watches movies on the streaming app Kanopy, which requires a library card or university login.

Her library card also provides her with free access to The New York Times online, a perk that extends beyond her neighborhood. For example, The New York Public Library began offering visitors free access to The New York Times in January if they accessed the digital newspaper while on the library’s Wi-Fi network.

Rivera said she spends a lot of time at her local library.

“It’s somewhere I can go where I’m not expected to spend money,” she said. “If I were to go to a coffee shop just to get out and do something, the expectation would be to buy a coffee, even though I probably didn’t want a coffee.”

Librarians and library-goers across the country told NPR that financial pressures are drawing consumers into libraries. The pattern is familiar to library staff: When money gets tight, demand for their services grows.

Americans are cutting spending on optional items as they try to afford the necessities. A Pew Research Center report released last month found that 44% of Americans struggled to afford at least one basic necessity, like medical care or housing, over the previous year.

Meanwhile, 70% said they had trimmed spending on nonessentials like dining out and streaming subscriptions across the same time period. Pew Research conducted the survey in late August, and about 5,000 Americans responded.

Several of the nation’s largest public library systems showed NPR data reflecting recent increases in membership. More library-goers are also borrowing materials and attending events.

Maria McCauley, president of the American Library Association, said in an interview with NPR that she attributes some of that growth to “heightened awareness” of what libraries offer.

Libraries “are especially popular right now, I think, because people are aware that everything that we provide is free or very low cost,” McCauley said.

Shoppers looking to cut costs are rediscovering an old favorite location in their neighborhoods: their local library.

According to a new report from NPR, consumers are returning to the library to get more without stretching their paychecks.

Vail Has Free On-Mountain Grills— Here’s Where to Find Them

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Skiing isn’t exactly a budget-friendly sport. Between lift tickets, equipment rentals, and overpriced on-mountain meals, the costs can add up quickly. But tucked away at one of Colorado’s most famous ski resorts is an unexpected way to save money on lunch. Instead of spending a small fortune at an on-mountain restaurant, skiers can bring their own meats and grill them slopeside.

My husband and I have done exactly that; we packed our own meat for a midday cookout between runs at Vail Ski Resort. It’s one of those experiences that feels almost too good to be true, especially at a resort where even a casual lunch comes with a price.

And while saving money is certainly part of the appeal, there’s something special about taking a break from skiing to grill your own lunch surrounded by the Rocky Mountains.

Located in Blue Sky Basin, Belle’s Camp offers a different kind of dining experience. Rather than ordering from a menu, guests can bring their own food and use the public grills to prepare lunch.

“Belle’s Camp is meant to be a different kind of on-mountain dining experience,” says Kevin Delonay, Vail Mountain’s senior director of food and beverage. “Guests bring their own food, gather with friends and family, and make lunch part of their day in Blue Sky Basin.”

It’s a surprisingly simple concept. Pack whatever you’d like to eat, carry it with you while skiing, and head to Belle’s Camp when you’re ready for lunch. Burgers, hot dogs, and steaks are all possibilities, although you’ll want to consider how easily your food can be transported in a backpack.

Visitors should also pack whatever they need to enjoy their meal, including any necessary utensils, condiments, and other supplies. Since you’re carrying everything onto the mountain, keeping things simple can make the experience much easier.

The grills are available during Belle’s Camp operating hours when Blue Sky Basin is open, although availability is subject to mountain operations and weather conditions.

“There are plenty of places at Vail where we cook the meal for you,” Delonay says. “Belle’s is special because we provide the setting and guests create the experience themselves.”

One of the biggest advantages of grilling your own lunch is the potential savings, especially for families or groups skiing together.

At Vail, a typical on-mountain cafeteria lunch can cost around $20 per person, while a more upscale dining experience can cost considerably more. At The 10th, Vail’s full-service restaurant at the top of Gondola One, a Wagyu cheeseburger is listed at $45. For a family of four, a lunch on the mountain could add up to $80 to $180 before drinks or other extras.

Keep reading: Everything You Need To Know About The Hottest New Ski Lunch Trend

Instead, visitors can stop at a grocery store before their ski day and stock up on ingredients for a fraction of the cost. For example, a pack of hot dogs, buns, and a few toppings could feed several people for around $15 to $20. Burgers are another relatively inexpensive option, while those looking to make lunch more of an occasion can bring steaks.

Of course, the exact savings depend on what you purchase and how many people are in your group. But for families or friends already spending hundreds of dollars on lift tickets, rentals, and other ski expenses, packing lunch is one of the easiest ways to cut costs.

And unlike eating a cold sandwich on a chairlift, grilling your own meal gives you a chance to sit down, relax, and enjoy a warm meal on the mountain with a new sense of appreciation.

Despite being one of the resort’s more unusual experiences, Belle’s Camp isn’t necessarily something every first-time visitor knows about.

According to Delonay, much of its popularity has developed organically, with skiers introducing their friends and family to the experience.

“Belle’s Camp has really grown through word of mouth, and I think that’s part of its charm,” he says. “People discover it, bring their friends back and start their own traditions.”

That’s part of what makes the experience so memorable. Some visitors might stop for a quick burger between runs, while others turn lunch into a full-blown gathering with friends.

“We see everything from a simple lunch with friends to groups that really turn it into an occasion,” Delonay says. “That creativity is part of what makes Belle’s so much fun.”

For my husband and me, grilling lunch at Belle’s Camp was a fun change from the typical ski resort dining experience. It’s not every day you get to cook your own meal in the middle of one of the country’s most famous ski resorts.

If you love grilling your own lunch on the mountain, Vail isn’t your only option in Colorado.

Loveland Ski Area, near the Continental Divide, also lets skiers bring their own food and cook lunch without visiting a traditional restaurant.

The resort has four on-mountain cabins that the public can use for free, each equipped with propane grills. Visitors are welcome and encouraged to pack their own food and prepare a meal during their ski day.

It’s a particularly appealing option for families and groups who want a warm lunch without paying on-mountain restaurant prices. But beyond the savings, it’s also a fun way to slow down and enjoy a different side of skiing.

After all, some of the most memorable moments on the mountain don’t happen on the slopes. Sometimes, they’re as simple as grilling lunch with friends and family between runs.

Mortgage Mix: Buyers Turning to ARMs Due to Elevated Rates; Compass Expands Its Mortgage Business

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Editor’s Note: The Mortgage Mix is RISMedia’s highlight reel of need-to-know mortgage-industry happenings. Watch for it every other Friday afternoon.

The numbers: While mortgage rates had hit a peak and were nearing the high-end of the 7% range, they have now decreased for the third straight week to 7.18% for the 30-year fixed-rate mortgage and 6.57% for the 15-year fixed-rate mortgage. Notably, this was the lowest level seen in over a month.

What this means: A stronger jobs report was partially attributed to this week’s improvement, as Realtor.com® Senior Economist Hannah Jones said that “while solid hiring supports housing demand over time, a stronger-than-expected report could also lift bond yields and slow the pace of rate declines in the near term.

The “blue wave” that occurred in the elections this past Tuesday may also contribute to further improvements down the road, as Jones noted that “many newly elected representatives have voiced discontent with the ongoing conflict in the Middle East, which has contributed to rising inflation, so any progress toward resolution could mean lower inflation and softer mortgage rates ahead.”

Using their ARMs? Despite a come down, many buyers are still holding off from the market due to rates remaining over 7%. Mortgage applications this week were down 1.2%.

With the fixed-rate mortgage elevated, more buyers have turned to adjustable rate mortgages (ARMs) instead.

“As we have highlighted in recent weeks, more borrowers are choosing ARMs in this environment to lower their initial payments, with the ARM share at its highest level since September 2025,” said Mike Fratantoni, the Mortgage Bankers Association’s SVP and chief economist.

A Better battle comes to an end: After a tumultuous struggle between the lender and its founder, Better.com has dropped its federal securities lawsuit against Vishal Garg, who was ousted in August. The case was dismissed with prejudice, meaning it cannot be refiled.

The lender had been suing Garg for allegedly violating federal securities laws, arguing that he did not engage in the proper process to solicit shareholders in a bid to regain his position.

That bid ended up being successful, with Garg back in a leadership role.

Compass takes mortgage JV to the big leagues: Expanding its mortgage business to become a top-10 lender, Compass announced a merger between its own OriginPoint mortgage platform and Rate’s Guaranteed Rate Affinity to unite the two mortgage ventures under the OriginPoint brand.

Guaranteed Rate Affinity was founded in partnership with Anywhere Real Estate back in 2017, making the merger an unsurprising move due to Compass’s acquisition of Anywhere last year.

The merger expands OriginPoint’s resources and geographic reach, the companies noted in a release, while backing the business with Rate’s AI technology, products and lending platform.

Claudia Larsen is a senior editor for RISMedia.

Tegan and Sara Celebrate the Season With ‘I Love Christmas’ and ‘A December to Remember’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tegan and Sara are getting into the holiday spirit with not one, but two Christmas-themed songs, “I Love Christmas” and “A December to Remember.”

In the video for “I Love Christmas” the Quin sisters acknowledge not everyone is into the holiday, but they have enough cheer to make up for those who aren’t feeling it. “I love Christmas, even if you don’t/I can love it for us, even if you won’t,” they sing on the chorus. “I love the music and the weather and the presents, too/I know you hate this time of year, it makes you blue/Let me love it for you.”

The adoration for the season is not mere talk. “I’m a Christmas fanatic. I have three Christmas trees,” she says. “I watch a Christmas movie every single day in December and listen exclusively to Christmas music.” Tegan said in a statement. “Like most sane people, I actually prefer the anticipation of Christmas to Christmas itself. By Christmas afternoon, I’m usually pretty depressed.”

For “A December to Remember,” Tegan and Sara take turns grabbing stuff from a refrigerator as they sing about wrapping gifts and watching Home Alone together, and making other joyful memories. “You work to keep my spirits bright/You know that this is the time of the year I love, so you pull out all the stops and give me a December to remember with 25 reasons to cheer” goes the chorus. The visual culminates with the sisters clad in Christmas sweaters toasting champagne.

The songs hailed from a project with Kate Cooper penned around the time of Tegan and Sara’s 2024 graphic novel, Crush. After Tegan finished penning Merry Christmess, her first middle-grade novel that arrived in September, she said that she decided to revisit the songs. Trending Stories A Jesuit Priest Explains How He Facilitated Bob Dylan’s Meeting With the Pope Appeals Court Says Lawsuit Over ‘The Pitt’ Can Proceed, Cites ‘Numerous’ Similarities to ‘ER’ Adam Lambert on His Future With Queen, New Music, and Why the Show Must Go On Pink Floyd Mark ‘Animals’ 50th Anniversary With Reissue Featuring Unreleased Music

Tegan acknowledges the time of year is not everyone’s favorite. “A lot of people think Christmas is a commercialized, capitalist pain in the ass, but I genuinely love the traditions, the nostalgia, and the excuse to spend time with people you love making new memories,” she said, but added: “These songs are for people who love Christmas, but also for people who don’t,” she adds. “As I sing in ‘I Love Christmas,’ even if you hate Christmas (these songs), I’m happy to love it for you.”

In September, they dropped new single “Our Year” featuring Lights and Felix Cartal, their first new material since 2022’s Crybaby. Next fall, they embark on a tour with Hilary Duff for select U.S. dates.

Miguel Delaney: Man Utd Keen on Replacing Carrick With Sebastian Hoeness

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Manchester United's dramatic victory in midweek has given Michael Carrick a stay of execution but new details have emerged on his potential replacement.

The embattled Red Devils came back from two down to beat Roma 3-2 in the Champions League on Tuesday, ending a dreadful run of form which had seen them go without a win in any competition since defeating Sabah almost a month ago.

While the result has lifted the mood around Carrick and his squad, it remains only the first win in 10, and the Premier League picture remains dire.

United are 17th in the table ahead of the visit of Aston Villa on Sunday and haven't beaten anybody in the top flight since Ipswich at the end of August.

If that remains the club's only league win heading into the next international break the possibility that Carrick loses his job remains very real, although a lack of conviction over what to do about his future appears to be prolonging the situation.

Despite long-term links to former Newcastle boss Eddie Howe, the Englishman reportedly doesn't convince the powers that be at Old Trafford as much as widely believed, according to the Independent's Miguel Delaney.

Nobody within the hierarchy in Manchester is apparently prepared to make a decision on how to proceed with the manager position, amid confusion over who should be the proper replacement.

In that context, Sebastian Hoeness at Stuttgart has emerged as an option with some backing, with the German reported to be "highly rated" at Old Trafford.

However, tempering that positive view is the disastrous reign of Carrick's predecessor as permanent manager, Ruben Amorim, whose time in charge of United began almost exactly two years ago but ended in ignominy last January after 14 months in charge.

The Portuguese, now managing AC Milan, arrived from Sporting as a two-time league winner in his home country and with a reputation as one of the best young managers in Europe.

But, having been pushed to join mid-season after the dismissal of Erik ten Hag, he was never able to adapt his methods to the Premier League and spent the rest of the campaign flirting with relegation, before losing the Europa League final to fellow league strugglers Tottenham.

Hoeness took over at Stuttgart with the club bottom of the Bundesliga in 2023 and transformed their fortunes. After avoiding relegation he finished second in his first full season, returning the club to the Champions League after well over a decade, and has reached two DFB Pokal finals since, winning one.

As such, he currently has a similar reputation as Amorim enjoyed prior to his nightmare in England, but it is seen as a similarly "huge" leap from Stuttgart to Manchester United.

Like Amorim, he has favoured a 3-4-3 system in recent weeks, which is very similar to Amorim, although he has also used a 4-2-3-1 formation on plenty of occasions too.

The lack of forward planning among the club's decision makers has compounded issues for successive managers and the current indecision potentially risks doing more of the same.

Backing Ten Hag despite concerns over his long-term suitability blew up in United's face, leading them to pressure Amorim to take over a squad built in his predecessor's image without a summer to implement his ideas.

The Portuguese never recovered and when Carrick led an Ole Gunnar Solskjaer-style revival last term, he was rewarded with the permanent job just like the Norwegian, only for things to fall apart this season.

Unless the Roma victory is the start of an unlikely rise from the ashes of this campaign, then the next manager, whoever it may be, faces an ever-growing task of turning the club around.

He played with Scott Parker at Charlton Athletic - Then he was jailed for £15m fraud

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Centre-back Richard Rufus was in the same Charlton Athletic team as Scott Parker, but despite an eleven-year career at The Valley, his post-retirement life has been one misstep after another.

The life of the professional footballer can be an extremely insecure one. All players are one serious injury from their career being over, while a downturn in form could mean that their next contract could be their last.

A lucky few make it through to their mid-to-late thirties without any mishaps, but there's also a number who don't even make it that far, and in the case of the former Charlton Athletic defender Richard Rufus, a series of injuries resulted in his career ending at just 29 years of age.

But Rufus's post-playing career has not been a good one. The former centre-back has repeatedly found himself in trouble with the law, to an extent that, despite having been inducted into Charlton's hall of fame in 2013, he's at risk of becoming better known for this post-career proclivities than he was for his talents on the pitch.

As a player, Richard Rufus was the definition of a one-club man. Born in Lewisham in 1975, he joined Charlton Athletic as a youth and progressed through the club's academy, making his debut for them as a 20-year-old against Wolverhampton Wanderers in April 1995.

He would go on to spend 11 years at The Valley, and the early years of his career suggested that he could go on to get to the very top of the game. He was selected six times for the England under-21s over the course of 1996 and 1997, and by this time he was an established face at the heart of the Charlton central defence.

In 1998, he was part of the Charlton team which returned to the top-flight for the first time since 1990, though his Premier League debut against Newcastle United in August 1998 only lasted 25 minutes before he was sent off, and Charlton were relegated back to what was then still known as the First Division again.

On this occasion, though, Charlton bounced straight back up at the end of the 1999-2000 season, winning the First Division title, with Rufus scoring six goals in 44 appearances for them. His team-mate at the centre of the Charlton defence for a while in the early 2000s, Gary Rowett, later stated that Rufus was "on the radar" for a full England call-up by this time.

Among the other players in the Charlton team of that time was Scott Parker, who would go on to hit considerable heights with West Ham United, Spurs and Fulham, and who's since made a successful career for himself as a manager.

Charlton Athletic made significant profit on Darren Bent and Scott Parker.

But while the new century started explosively well for Rufus and his club, it didn't take long for injury problems to start mounting up. Rufus made 32 appearances for Charlton in the 2001-02 season, but the following season he was limited to just ten as the result of a knee injury.

The start of the 2003-04 season suggested that he had recovered from his knee problems, but behind the scenes the injury returned, and throughout the year he underwent a series of operations. It looked briefly as though he was back when he appeared on their bench for a League Cup match against Luton Town in September 2003 which Charlton won on penalty kicks.

But in truth, the injury hadn't fully cleared, and after one final operation carried out by the renowned orthopedic surgeon Richard Steadman in the USA failed, he was forced to retire from the professional game at the age of just 29 in the summer of 2004. In total, he made 254 appearances for Charlton over 11 years, scoring twelve goals. In 2013, he was inducted into the club's hall of fame.

Rufus's post-playing life has been blighted by a succession of run-ins with the law. The problems started in October 2013 when, having set himself up as a financial advisor, he was declared bankrupt with debts totalling £6 million. Among his creditors were the Pentecostal church of which he was a member, which lost £5m, the equivalent to half of a £10.1 million windfall they'd received from the sale of land in Hackney, as well as family members. Rufus had promised his church a 72% return on their investment, but they only managed to claw back £1.4 million from it.

From here on, things only seemed to spiral. By May 2015, his bankruptcy had been discharged, but he was in court again following an investigation into misappropriation of funds and the unregulated taking of deposits. It was established that Rufus had operated a £16m Ponzi scheme involving more than 100 investors, including members of his family and congregation members of churches he had attended, pocketing more than £3 million. He received a 15-year bankruptcy restriction order for his troubles.

The matter ended up being treated as a criminal matter, and in 2019 he was charged with fraud, money laundering and carrying out a regulated activity without authorisation. It took a long time for the case to come to trial - it wasn't heard until November 2022 - but in January 2023 the former player was convicted and sentenced to seven-and-a-half years in prison. It was heard that Rufus had used his former career as a professional footballer to project a status of wealth while living off the money that he had promised to invest. In total, he paid back £7.4 million of investors’ money and lost them £8.7 million.

Both Gary Rowett and Scott Parker have gone on to have successful careers in management. Rowett has managed at eight different EFL clubs, while Parker has won promotion from the Championship three times and has recently landed a plum job at West Ham United. In stark contrast to that, though, the sort of management that Richard Rufus went into once his playing career ended up being of a very different sort, and he's lost pretty much everything as a result.

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Paraguay Protests Over Bank Lockout Intensify as Impeachment Vote Nears

Thousands of Paraguayans marched through Asunción this week demanding their money back after Ueno Bank, a digital lender, locked about 3 million customers out of their accounts, according.

That is about 60% of Paraguay’s adults. The bank also held $800 million to $850 million in public money, including state pension and health funds, according to the AP, so the fallout reaches the pension system.

Anger boiled over Thursday night outside Ueno’s headquarters in Asunción, where savers burned debit and credit cards. Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas, a former presidential candidate, tried to join and was booed, shoved, and forced out.

Protesters shouted “divisor” and “oportunista,” meaning divider and opportunist, accusing him of trying to profit politically from public rage, according to the same report.

Diario Hoy reported that protesters also blamed him for the Senate’s current makeup, saying Cubas put Zenaida Delgado and Javier Vera on his party’s legislative ticket, the slate of candidates voters choose from, and that both joined the government’s side soon after taking office.

#Nacionales | 😱 Echan a "Payo" Cubas de la manifestación

🔹 Paraguayo “Payo” Cubas fue rechazado por los manifestantes cuando intentó acercarse a la protesta. Los presentes lo increparon y le gritaron “divisor” y “oportunista”.

➡️ Además, cuestionaron su responsabilidad en el… pic.twitter.com/odHzVvGPO3

— Universo 970 AM (@Universo970py) November 6, 2026

President Santiago Peña, a shareholder in Ueno’s holding company until March 2025, submitted his resignation Thursday, but a joint session of Congress called to accept it adjourned without a quorum, according to La Nación. He then said he would face impeachment—a trial in Congress to remove a president—and defend himself.

The lower house had already voted 78-0 to open impeachment, and on Friday the Senate rejected his request for 72 extra hours to prepare his defense. The Senate planned a removal vote for Friday night, ABC Color reported, with Vice President Pedro Alliana next in line.

Horacio Cartes, the Colorado Party leader and Peña’s former political mentor, withdrew his support and backed the impeachment. “We will not be on the side of the plunder of public, private and retirees’ funds,” Cartes said.

Interior Minister Enrique Riera and central bank president Carlos Carvallo have resigned, and eight Ueno executives have been arrested, according to La Nación’s Friday update. Prosecutors also opened a criminal investigation into Peña’s possible responsibility for the bank’s irregularities, which he denies.

Police used water cannons and tear gas on marchers near Congress, and a new law requires most deposits to be returned within five business days, though protesters have not been placated.

The central bank says its deposit guarantee covers up to 228.3 million guaraníes per person, about $36,000, but as Decrypt previously reported, the first payouts will go only to accounts with up to 10 million guaraníes (around $1,600) and no debts to Ueno.

Bitcoiners, of course, rushed to use Paraguay’s banking crisis as an example on the benefits of decentralization and self custody, and why the “Bitcoin fixes this” motto is still relevant in today’s financial system. “Bitcoin adoption at a nation-state level can mitigate these types of situations,” said JAN3 founder Samson Mow. “If BTC is available to the people as a parallel rail, then systemic banking failures have far less impact.”

“Bitcoin matters now more than ever. Look at Paraguay today,” wrote Stani Kulechov, founder of the DeFi protocol Aave.

The Federation of Educators of Paraguay has called a national strike and march for Monday, Nov. 9, Diario Hoy reported.

Zcash’s Game-Changing NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Slashing Block Times 3X as $5,000 ZEC Rally Comes Into Focus

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Zcash (ZEC) has just scored a major network milestone after activating its highly anticipated NU7 upgrade early Friday, introducing a significant improvement that cuts the blockchain’s target block interval from 75 seconds to just 25 seconds. The threefold increase in block production speed marks a notable step forward for the privacy-focused cryptocurrency, but with market watchers eyeing an ambitious $5,000 price target, could the latest network breakthrough provide the catalyst ZEC bulls have been waiting for?

Zcash has officially entered a new chapter after its landmark NU7 network upgrade went live at block 3,543,405 early Friday, activating the Network Sustainability Mechanism (NSM). The major change introduces a new approach to funding the privacy-focused blockchain’s long-term security as block rewards gradually decline. Celebrating the milestone, Zcash’s official account declared that “a new era of Zcash is here.”

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

The Network Sustainability Mechanism addresses a long-term challenge that Zcash shares with Bitcoin: how to keep its network secure as mining rewards decline with each halving. As block subsidies become smaller, both networks face questions about whether transaction fees alone can provide enough incentive for miners to continue securing the blockchain.

Under the new mechanism, 60% of transaction fees from each block goes into a publicly visible reserve, while miners receive the remaining 40%. The approach creates a dedicated funding pool to support Zcash’s long-term security and sustainability while preserving its hard-capped supply of 21 million ZEC.

The reserved fees are scheduled to be gradually reissued through block rewards starting at block 8,939,664, expected in 2031, with the recycled funds distributed along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

NU7 introduces several other notable changes, including a reduction in Zcash’s target block interval from 75 seconds to just 25 seconds, according to the Zcash Foundation. The upgrade also retires Sprout, the network’s original privacy pool launched in 2016, leaving approximately $16 million worth of ZEC locked in the pool. However, faster block production comes with a trade-off: it increases the likelihood of orphan blocks, which are valid blocks discarded by the network when another miner’s block reaches the rest of the network first.

NU7 also introduces a cap on the amount of shielded transaction activity that can be included in each block, reducing the volume of data wallets need to scan when searching for incoming payments. This is expected to make wallet synchronization more efficient and improve the overall user experience.

Despite the major network upgrade, ZEC has yet to register a strong bullish market reaction. The cryptocurrency was down 0.8% over the past 24 hours, trading at $1,313 at press time, as broader weakness across the crypto market weighed on prices.

Despite the recent price pullback, some prominent crypto investors remain bullish on Zcash’s longer-term prospects. Chris Burniske, a renowned investor known for his early interest in Solana (SOL), pointed to the ZEC/BTC chart as the key indicator to watch, describing it as “still the only chart that matters as it shows what comes next.”

Meanwhile, Zcash co-founder Eli Ben-Sasson remains firm on his ambitious price projections, even as ZEC faces renewed selling pressure. As ZyCrypto previously reported, Ben-Sasson doubled down on his prediction that the privacy-centric cryptocurrency could reach $5,000 before the end of 2026, describing a move to $2,000 as a “no-brainer.” He also revealed that he had backed the $2,000 target through OffMarket, a privacy-focused platform linked to prediction market Polymarket.

Godzilla Minus Zero's Surprise Villain And Its Franchise History Explained

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This article contains major spoilers for "Godzilla Minus Zero."

The King of the Monsters is back, and this time, he's not alone. Director Takashi Yamazaki has returned with a follow-up to his Oscar-winning smash hit "Godzilla Minus One" in the form of "Godzilla Minus Zero." Topping the film's predecessor was never going to be easy, but Yamazaki brought a not-so-secret weapon: King Ghidorah.

The King Ghidorah design for "Godzilla Minus Zero" leaked online thanks to merchandise, despite Toho trying to keep the monster's reveal a secret from the viewing public. But now, the cat is out of the bag, and this movie winds up being a showdown between Godzilla and one of his oldest foes. And much in the same way that Yamazaki reinvented Godzilla in "Minus One," he very much puts his own stamp on Ghidorah in "Minus Zero."

Anyone who has seen the sequel undoubtedly knows that King Ghidorah is a wildly powerful, foreboding kaiju. The design of the monster is very faithful to what fans of the franchise have known for years, but he's also modernized with some terrifying additions, including some wild new abilities that lead to some downright jaw-dropping action sequences. "Godzilla Minus Zero" is rated R, and that allows Yamazaki to not hold back in terms of the destruction and chaos caused by this hulking beast.

As even casual "Godzilla" fans are surely aware, this is far from the first time that Ghidorah has gone toe-to-toe with the King himself. So, in light of his latest appearance, it's worth looking back at the monster's history, how he's evolved over the years, and how this movie puts a new twist on him.

King Ghidorah first appeared on-screen in 1964's "Ghidorah, the "Three-Headed Monster," one of the best "Godzilla" movies of all time. By that point, the "Godzilla" franchise had become rather popular, and the titular monster was no longer alone. He'd faced off against Anguirus, King Kong, and Mothra. So, Ghidorah was far from the first kaiju battle for Godzilla, but he set a new high bar and instantly asserted himself as Godzilla's most powerful foe.

In terms of his origins, King Ghidorah is an alien monster who wiped out an alien race on Venus and came to Earth on a meteor. Ghidorah was such a formidable threat that the only way he could be defeated was to unite Godzilla, Mothra, and Rodan together against him. Ghidorah is truly one of the only monsters that might be scarier than Godzilla. This was essentially the first time that Godzilla went from villain to humanity's savior (although that line was already starting to blur by then).

Ghidorah quickly became a staple of the property. He re-appeared a year later in 1965's "Invasion of the Astro-Monster" and then again in 1968's "Destroy All Monsters," which features one of the most iconic "Godzilla" movie moments (Ghidorah getting stomped out by a group of kaiju). Ghidorah has since come back for every era of the franchise, including 1991's "Godzilla vs. King Ghidorah" during the Heisei era (which also introduced Mecha-King Ghidorah).

He later appeared in the Millennium era in 2001's chaotic "Godzilla, Mothra and King Ghidorah: Giant Monsters All-Out Attack" and in the MonsterVerse in 2019's "Godzilla: King of the Monsters." Rivaled perhaps only by Mothra, Ghidorah truly is Godzilla's most notorious foe. And where Mothra is often an ally, Ghidorah is as bad as they come.

On the one hand, Takashi Yamazaki is very faithful to King Ghidorah in "Godzilla Minus Zero." At the same time, though, he makes the monster feel fresh again with a terrifying makeover, one that fits in very well with the universe he set up in "Minus One." Ghidorah still shoots lightning bolts from his mouth, has that gold, scaly skin and those three dangerous heads, and remains massive. But there's an evolution to the kaiju here that makes this version particularly terrifying.

The very first scene of the movie shows us Ghidorah in a new form, namely what looks, for all intents and purposes, like a dragon with just one head. His design would be right at home in "Reign of Fire" (one of many fantasy movies that didn't deserve to flop at the box office). But I digress. After this initial attack, we learn that Ghidorah arrived on Earth via a meteor, keeping his alien origins intact.

Yamazaki's big change comes when Japan tries to kill a dormant Ghidorah. After dropping a bomb on him and impaling him so that he'll be struck with lightning, Ghidorah evolves into his final, more familiar form. The biggest change is his horrifying ability to use gravity beams, literally lifting towns into the sky and dropping them down to Earth.

The big climax relies on the film's humans forcing Godzilla to fight Ghidorah in a winner-take-all grudge match. Godzilla wins by blowing Ghidorah to pieces, but it isn't easy. The ending of "Godzilla Minus Zero" doesn't make it clear where the franchise goes from here, but it does leave some doors open. However, what isn't left dangling is any question as to who reigns supreme. At the end of the day, Godzilla is still the king.

The <i><em>Black Doves</em></i> Season 2 Finale Has an Insane Cliffhanger

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Let’s talk about Helen Webb and what it all means for season 3.

After a two-year wait, Netflix's Christmas crime caper Black Doves is finally back for its second outing. It's a messy delight that tests the loyalties of the central characters in ways the first season never dared and—both annoyingly and excitingly—ends on just as agonizing a cliffhanger.

In the first season, Elizabeth Reed (Sarah Lancashire) was something of a mystery: an ominous, witty controller of sorts who kept Helen Webb (Keira Knightley) and Sam Young (Ben Whishaw) on their toes. But this time around, Black Doves spends some time unravelling her past, as well as that of the titular intelligence agency: a backstory that informs and explains the major threat of the season, the German anarchist organization Kestrel.

Though the secrets reveal themselves slowly, a bombshell-filled (pun very much intended) finale crashes through a series of twists that—if you didn't see them coming (which I can't say I did, for some of them)—will really feel like a stab in the face. No, not the back, because unfortunately the answers were always lying right in front of us.

There's a lot to break down from that last episode, so read on for our recap of Black Doves season 2 and an explanation of the ending—including that twist that'll probably have a drastic impact on where we'll be at the beginning of season 3.

The penultimate episode ended with a bunch of crossed wires, conflicting contracts, and potential deaths. Eleanor and Williams have taken out paid hits on Laila and Sam, while Sam's final target in his prison-break contract with Kestrel is none other than Reed (after episode 4 revealed that she is in fact the mysterious “Wildflower” of the files she tried to take from Alexi Chernov).

The main action kicks off when the precious international peace summit that Wallace (Andrew Buchan) has spent the whole season planning gets upended before it even starts. Wallace and Helen's cab is intercepted and chased, while U. S. defense envoy Cecile Mason (Neve Campbell) and recently widowed Russian envoy Katia Chernov (Sylvia Hoeks) are captured by strange armed men.

When Patrick (Sam Riley), the Kestrel man who's been blackmailing Sam all season, escorts Reed back into the Black Doves office with a gun to her head, it quickly becomes clear that 'Wildflower', or Reed, is the only target Kestrel wants brought in alive. Or, at least, whoever's running it does.

And there's the twist: after a horrid little hostage situation in the office results in the deaths of quite a few senior Black Doves and Mason, Katia reveals that she is in fact the head of Kestrel and, in turn, Vita—the long-lost, abandoned daughter that Reed left in Berlin after killing her fake husband and running off (ouch).

Dani, the nuisance of a secretary Reed planted in Wallace's office to seduce him and potentially replace Helen, has ended up being recruited by Kestrel. But she doesn't last long after a game of grenade hot potato played by Eleanor, Williams, and Laila ends with Dani being caught in a lift with it. Even Reed's capable of mistakes.

Meanwhile, over in the car park where Helen and Wallace end up after fleeing, Mr. Prime Minister finally decides to question his wife about her past and her secrets. Obviously, she lies again—until the situation shifts a little.

Helen receives a series of ominous messages and threatening instructions, first over text, then through a discreet earpiece planted in her car. Katia, whom Helen very quickly realizes is not an ally, instructs her to kill Wallace and turn herself in to MI5. But what's the leverage, you ask?

Marie, the housekeeper who was teased as a Black Dove at the end of the first season, has in fact been working for Kestrel this whole time. If you're a babysitter to the Prime Minister's spy wife, who's been planted by a capitalist intelligence agency, but you're working for a German anarchist organization, does that make you a double or a triple agent? A nightmare for Mrs Webb, more than anything.

Helen tricks Katia over the phone by shooting past Wallace towards the window, explains (on a surface level) that the children are in danger and she needs to hijack the car, and darts off with Sam to rescue the children from Marie.

After confronting Marie, Helen shoots her in the head and flees the scene, while Reed reconciles (not quite) with her bleeding-out daughter in her last moments. Kestrel's down and all is well? Obviously not, because it's never that simple.

MI5 is even more suspicious of Helen than ever before, but Wallace is covering for her. Conteh, as one of the few surviving senior members of the Black Doves, restores Reed to her former standing.

Helen and Sam reconcile properly after their heated argument during the first Reed rescue mission of the season, and he reveals that he's going to leave the country once again. After they part, a strange man bumps into Helen and, BOOM, final twist! Patrick has escaped in perfect condition and Kestrel is still going strong. He leaves Helen with his card, more as a threat than anything else.

Reed and Helen agree to increase Helen's standing and role within the Black Doves, meaning that she'll be more than just an intel-grabber going forward, and putting her in a pretty good spot work-wise. However, things aren't quite so simple at home.

Wallace confronts Helen once again about her real name (Lily Morris), and the finale ends with what appears to be the beginning of a conversation about her past. But how much is Helen actually going to tell Wallace? There's only so much she can give away without completely compromising her position, but she'll have to say enough to regain his trust. We'll find out in two years, I suppose.

OpenAI Secures $11B in Initial Funding at $1.25T Valuation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Reuters reported on November 6, 2026 that OpenAI had closed on an initial $11 billion in new funding, the first part of a round valuing the company at $1.25 trillion, citing a source familiar with the matter.

The amount raised so far represents only a portion of OpenAI’s original target of up to $30 billion. Two investors said the valuation had come down from the $1.5 trillion level the company initially sought, and that SoftBank and MGX participated. The financing remains an initial close within a larger plan, with the original fundraising target unfinished.

The funding landed after a month of cooling sentiment toward AI companies. Some investors valued Anthropic’s planned initial public offering at about $1.5 trillion, below earlier expectations of roughly $2 trillion. Those reduced expectations provide the immediate market context for OpenAI’s own lower valuation ambition.

In its March 31, 2026 funding announcement, OpenAI said it had closed a previous round with $122 billion in committed capital at an $852 billion post-money valuation. Amazon, NVIDIA and SoftBank anchored that financing, with continued participation from Microsoft. SoftBank co-led alongside a16z, D. E. Shaw Ventures, MGX, TPG and accounts advised by T. Rowe Price Associates. SoftBank and MGX were already part of OpenAI’s investor base, so their reported participation extends existing relationships.

OpenAI said the March round raised more than $3 billion from individual investors through bank channels, the first time it had extended participation through that route. The company expanded its revolving credit facility to approximately $4.7 billion, which remained undrawn at that round’s close. Together, those details show the breadth of capital channels OpenAI had assembled by March.

OpenAI’s March announcement also detailed the business behind its capital requirements. The company said it was generating $2 billion in revenue per month at that time, with enterprise customers accounting for more than 40% of revenue, and projected that enterprise revenue would reach parity with consumer revenue by the end of 2026.

Its cloud partners include Microsoft, Oracle, AWS, CoreWeave and Google Cloud, while its chip strategy spans NVIDIA, AMD, AWS Trainium, Cerebras and an OpenAI chip developed with Broadcom. The company also works with Oracle, SBE and SoftBank on data centers. That portfolio puts the fundraising in operational context. OpenAI’s stated rationale is that no single architecture can efficiently meet the needs of the entire AI frontier, making infrastructure breadth central to its deployment strategy.

The partial close and reduced valuation ambition show that OpenAI’s operating scale does not eliminate investor scrutiny. For the remainder of the round, the central tension is between a compute strategy that demands substantial capital and an AI market where valuation expectations have cooled.

Crypto Slump Could Be Far From Over, Barclays Analyst Warns

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Another sell-off could be on the way for crypto after a leading strategist at Barclays warned that the recent U.S. stock market rally may not last.

There are more signs of trouble in the cryptocurrency market, with a leading strategist at Barclays warning that the recent rebound in U.S. stocks may not last.

Alexander Altmann, the global head of equities tactical strategies at Barclays, is now somewhat skeptical of the strength of the stock market rebound after the brutal sell-off that has also dragged cryptocurrencies lower.

He lists factors such as negative market breadth, mediocre growth stock performance, a modest rebound in neocloud stocks, the ongoing crypto slump, and a flat quality factor.

The concerns from Altmann come amid increasing worries that the AI investment frenzy that has been a key driver of U.S. equity market performance may not be sustainable.

Barclays strategist was one of the few Wall Street experts who questioned whether the U.S. midterm elections would bring lasting benefits to stocks.

Barclays has flagged the risk that investment strategies sensitive to volatility could help worsen the market decline.

According to Barclays models cited in Bloomberg’s report, volatility-control funds may be at risk of dumping as much as $135 billion of U.S. equity futures in the 24 to 48 hours following Thursday’s market mayhem.

On the Oct. 29 episode of Bloomberg’s Odd Lots podcast, Altmann also argued that investors are in a “denial phase” concerning the risks to the AI sector.

He pointed to growing financing pressure on neocloud companies that host computing resources for AI applications as a sign of early stress in the industry.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Rizwan is an experienced Crypto journalist with almost half a decade of experience covering everything related to the growing crypto industry — from price analysis to blockchain disruption. During this period, he’s authored more than 3,000 news articles for Coinpedia News.

Bitcoin price dropped below $75,000 today after falling more than 6% from the $80,000 area.

A 2023-like setup could support Bitcoin’s recovery if it reclaims the $82,000 and $87,400 levels.

Analyst spot that Bitcoin is testing weekly 50 MA support near $75,500, with $67,800 as the next key level.

Bitcoin, the pioneering cryptocurrency, has dropped below $75,000 for the first time since August, adding pressure to an already-weak crypto market. The drop comes after a sharp decline from the $80,000 level, raising a bigger question for traders.

Is Bitcoin entering another deeper correction, or could this setup mirror its 2023 recovery?

Bitcoin dropped roughly 6.4% from around $80,307 to nearly $74,800 on November 6 before recovering slightly toward $75,600. The fall came as technology, semiconductor, and AI stocks faced heavy selling, spreading pressure across global markets.

Another major trigger came from the latest US Initial Jobless Claims report. Initial claims unexpectedly fell to 189,000 against expectations of 197,000, showing continued strength in the US labor market.

The stronger-than-expected data reduced hopes for easier monetary policy, putting pressure on Bitcoin and other risk assets.

The sell-off also triggered heavy liquidations. According to CoinGlass, 111,345 traders were liquidated over 24 hours, with total losses reaching $427.18 million. Long positions accounted for nearly $327 million, or around 76.7% of the total.

Despite the drop, prominent crypto analyst Ash Crypto sees similarities between Bitcoin’s current weekly chart and its 2023 setup.

In 2023, Bitcoin traded between its weekly 50-week and 100-week moving averages before breaking above the range and starting a major rally. Now, BTC is again moving between these two averages after facing rejection near the 100-week moving average around $87,400.

Ash Crypto places the 50-week moving average near $75,500 as the first major support. The 200-week moving average around $67,800 is another level to watch if Bitcoin falls further.

The analyst also points to stronger buying demand below $75,000 compared with the previous low. He describes this as a positive difference in market demand and one of his strongest buy signals.

Bitcoin needs to hold above $75,500 to keep the current chart setup intact. If buyers defend this level, the price could first test resistance near $82,000.

A stronger recovery above $82,000 could bring the 100-week moving average near $87,400 back into focus.

The weekly RSI is around 50, still above the usual bear-market zone. However, the Stochastic RSI is weakening, leaving room for another drop.

Think Your Pack Is Heavy? This Hiker Carries 220 Pounds For Fun

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

On a Saturday in Slovakia’s Tatra Mountains, Regan Bridge stood in a line of tourists at the foot of a cliff studded with ladders and chains. Strapped to his back was his own 120-liter army pack, stuffed with 132 pounds of supplies. A carry strap ran across his forehead. “It was ugly,” he says.

He couldn’t get the pack up the ladder, so Bridge did what nobody was supposed to do and climbed the chains on the path designated for the descent, in full view of the local porters, who are famous for hauling extraordinary weights.

When he hikes back home in New Zealand, 54-year-old Bridge’s setup looks like a stunt. Toolboxes stacked high, a ladder, and up to 220 pounds adorn the back of the log truck driver. Up close, he is the opposite of a stuntman: He has no social media and makes no money from his hobby.

I write about ultralight gear, so on paper Bridge and I don’t have much in common. I carry light, while he hauls as much as he can. He gets jokes from passersby about bringing the whole house, and I get asked whether my boyfriend is carrying my sleeping bag. But we’ve both learned how easily others can misconstrue the way we pack, and how little that has to do with why we do it.

Having previously been involved in stone lifting, Bridge was no stranger to unusual tests of strength. His interest in carrying heavy loads in the backcountry began when he volunteered to transport 15 predator traps for a New Zealand conservation project. Typically, workers were only allowed to haul two at a time. Bridge asked for the rule to change; the organization waived its weight limit for him, and he cleared them all in two trips. The experience expanded his sense of what the human body could manage. “It grew my mindset,” he says, and he began looking for new ways to test it.

Since then, Bridge has slowly piled on more weight. His setup includes duplicate hiking gear, even multiple pairs of footwear, to maximize the load. The toolboxes hold water bottles, which he empties at the summit, roughly halving his load for the descent. He uses the ladder to get the pack on and off and to rest along the way; once it exceeds 110 pounds (50 kilograms), he can no longer lift it off the ground himself. Bridge admits he now favors steep, demanding dayhikes for the adrenaline rush; longer, multiday trips have lost their appeal.

After watching a documentary about Slovakia’s nosiči, the mountain porters who carry supplies to remote huts, Bridge traveled there hoping to experience it for himself. His wife of 25 years found a place that offered tourists the chance to carry supplies between huts in exchange for a free coffee. The suggested load was 44 pounds, or 20 kilos. “I looked at that and went, I ain’t carrying no 20 kilos.”

Instead, Bridge carried double the suggested weight up to the top hut on the first day, and was rewarded with coffee and rum, which, he says, “made that trip down really fun.” The following day his climb up the chains caught the porters’ attention. They offered him a turn carrying their traditional frame, laden with 110 pounds.

“They’re probably stepping out on a limb to give me a go,” he reflects. When he told them he did this for fun back home, they thought he was “a little bit crazy.” But among the porters, he was no oddity. He was “a dime a dozen.”

People can be quick to judge. Other hikers tell him it’s a waste of time; others warn his knees won’t last. “And I’m looking at them going, well, you’re tramping [hiking]. That’s a waste of time too.”

“I want to live. I want to do stuff,” he says. “If I have to get a knee done when I’m 60, I’ll get a knee done.”

He likes the history behind the porters’ work, but says, “I also like the space it puts me in while I’m doing it.” Climbing is his favorite part. “The climb just clears my brain a bit more, but I still have the chance to think about where I am, have a look at the view.” Descents are different; he’s too focused on not getting hurt. I’d pictured a stoic, silent grind; instead, he hauls to a soundtrack ranging from AC/DC to ’90s dance music. Asked what keeps him coming back, Bridge describes it as “just feeding the soul.”

Next, he wants to take 220 pounds (100 kilograms) up the 2,200 steps of the Sealy Tarns Track in Aoraki National Park. But his carefully balanced setup makes exposed climbs tricky.:“You put wind in there, it’s diabolical.”

Before we hung up, Regan pulled out the questions I’d sent ahead of our interview, which he’d printed, and went through them to ensure we’d covered everything. He wanted the facts right, because through translation, things can change.

“All of a sudden, I’m carrying 120 kilos (264 pounds),” he says. “I don’t want people to think that I’ve done something that I haven’t.”

He has no sponsors and no followers, yet he still wanted every number right—no flattering angle, no bigger feat, just a man who didn’t want credit for a kilo he hadn’t carried.

When the call ended, Bridge was off to spend an hour training, as he does six days a week. I’d asked him earlier whether there would ever be a peak, a weight where he’d say, “that’s enough”.

“One day I’m going to wake up and go, yep, I’m there. I’m happy,” he says. “And until then, it’s still going to continue to be fun.”

Matthias Jaissle caught in the storm as Newcastle struggle without European fix

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Panic building among fans as club visit Craven Cottage but head coach has resolute support of hierarchy

At least Matthias Jaissle has a sense of humour. “There’s a lot of storm at the moment,” reflected Newcastle’s head coach on Thursday. A wry smile suggested he was not referring to the north-east weather. Jaissle has seen his tactics, team selection, man management and entire philosophy rubbished in recent days but, on current evidence, appears to be holding his nerve. After all, it is not Jaissle’s fault that his appointment has coincided with supporters, players and directors struggling to adjust to life without Champions League involvement.

A disappointing 12th-place finish under Eddie Howe last season dictates that there are no more exciting trips to Barcelona, Paris and Marseille and, significantly, no much-needed boost to club finances. Indeed, Newcastle are finding life without last term’s continental fix so tricky that they travel to Fulham on Saturday with the disgruntled mindset of a passenger accustomed to flying business class who suddenly finds themselves stuck in a cramped, mid-row economy seat.

Even worse, many fans regard the trip to Craven Cottage as a full-blown relegation six-pointer. Given Newcastle are 13th on 11 points that, ostensibly, is somewhat hysterical. Yet anyone who has watched the bulk of Newcastle’s games this season will understand the reasons underpinning the sense of panic. And if Jaissle bears some responsibility for a run of four defeats in the past five games in all competitions, Newcastle’s unwanted club record-breaking run of 14 home league matches without a clean sheet stretches back into last season.

While Jaissle’s bloody-mindedness in persisting with a 4-2-3-1 formation when he lacks both a natural No 10 and pace and mobility in central midfield merits scrutiny, he was not responsible for close-season recruitment. Ross Wilson, the sporting director, signed eight players all aged 24 or under for a combined £262.9m. Only three – Amar Dedic, Nico González and Lukas Hornicek – are likely starters at present. A fourth, Sean Steur, an 18-year-old acquired from Ajax looks potentially top-drawer but Jaissle refuses to overplay him and risk attendant physical overload.

Fingers are duly being pointed at Wilson but the need to comply with football’s spending rules explains why Newcastle sold their three best players – Anthony Gordon, Sandro Tonali and Bruno Guimarães – last summer. The resultant vacuum left Wilson taking calculated gambles on inexperienced youngsters, including Matias Fernandez-Pardo, a £51m forward signed from Lille, Bazoumana Touré, a £42m winger signed from Hoffenheim, and Aladji Bamba, a £30m midfielder from Monaco. Newcastle’s Saudi Arabian owners hope they will prove unpolished gems with high resale values, but only one of those eight new faces, González, possesses Premier League experience. Throw in a youthful, relatively inexperienced manager and the risk is obvious.

While football’s financial regulations have triggered such wholesale player trading at a moment when Newcastle’s commercial revenues fail to match their ambitions, it did not help that Howe hoarded first-teamers, consistently resisting the opportunity to sell stars at peak value. Indeed, Alexander Isak only joined Liverpool for £125m last year after going on strike. The absence of a sporting director at that time left Howe and his nephew, Andy Howe, then a senior Newcastle recruitment executive, heavily involved in reinvesting the cash. The best part of £225m was poured into signing Nick Woltemade, Yoane Wissa, Anthony Elanga and Jacob Ramsey, but that quartet have collectively underwhelmed.

When a disillusioned, burned-out Howe quit three weeks before the new season, Wilson and Newcastle’s chief executive, David Hopkinson, had already lined up his successor. Jaissle’s much-vaunted attacking brand of so-called vertical football had secured two Asian Champions Leagues at Al-Ahli. On Tyneside, though, such ‘verticality’ has frequently turned into a series of optimistic long balls punctuating matches that frequently see Newcastle’s midfield overrun, forwards isolated and defenders exposed. Given that possession retention has long been a problem for a team arguably at their best when choreographed in contain-and-counter mode, Jaissle’s blueprint appears overly ambitious.

Newcastle’s formidable attacking pace menaces on the break but the manager’s plan during the 1-0 home defeat against Everton last Monday was to bisect the visiting lines courtesy of sharp incisive passing along the ground. It did not work and afterwards Jaissle criticised his players for disobeying orders, insisting “shooting long balls” had never been on the agenda. “It was not the plan and that is something you need to clearly mention,” he told reporters.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

By Thursday Jaissle looked considerably happier, insisting there was no discord between management and players. “Everyone believes in the long-term project,” he said. Jaissle had apparently been encouraged by his shot-shy players’ willingness to sign up for extra shooting practice but perhaps Dedic took such instruction too literally. On Wednesday afternoon, the Bosnia international sparked the involvement of an armed response unit after being spotted with a bow and arrow in the Northumberland countryside. The police swiftly accepted that the equipment was for “recreational use” but what has been described as a “naive misunderstanding” seems horribly emblematic of Newcastle’s season.

One thing remains clear: after describing Jaissle as “fire” and a “rock star” – not to mention paying Al-Ahli £9.5m to release him – Hopkinson will not sack the 38-year-old German lightly. He and Wilson are standing resolutely by their man.

“Everyone’s aware of, and realistic, about the journey ahead and where we are right now,” said Jaissle. “The nice part is that I get even more the feeling from the club of how committed they are.”

Alpine Linux and OpenSSH patch flaw that’s as bad as it can get

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

This dangerous Linux security hole must be patched as soon as possible.

The perfect nightmare scenario for a Linux server is when all an attacker needs to do is reach the server’s Secure Shell (SSH) port, waltz right in, and take it over. That’s what happened to Alpine Linux, the popular, lightweight Linux server. Adding insult to injury, this happened only days after Alpine Linux dodged a security hole-sized bullet to its GitLab implementation.

Also: Linux Mint 27 arrives next month, and I like it already: What to expect

How bad is it? As bad as it can get. Alpine Linux explained in its security advisory that if an attacker can reach a vulnerable server’s SSH port, they can get in “with one connection,” and the attack “works every time, and needs no retries.”

As security engineer Gregory Bryant explained in his LinkedIn post:

Most authentication bypasses come from complex logic chains, but yesterday’s Alpine Linux OpenSSH vulnerability (CVE-2026-123158) comes down to a single character: *.”

If you run Alpine Linux (or another musl-based distro) with the default openssh-server package (no Pluggable Authentication Modules (PAM)) and PasswordAuthentication enabled, an attacker can log into password-disabled, or key-only accounts simply by sending a password between 257 and 1024 bytes long.

Alpine Linux isn’t the only operating system that uses musl, a lightweight version of the standard C library (libc). Other operating systems that use musl and are potentially vulnerable include Adélie Linux; Void Linux; Sabotage Linux; OpenWrt, an Android variant,  and the Yocto Project, which is used to build embedded Linux distributions.

Also: This clever banking scam keeps phishing by faking its own demise

Wind River, which produces the commercial embedded Linux distro, Wind River Linux, is also looking into the issue. A company representative told me, “Wind River takes security seriously. We are actively reviewing the reported vulnerability and assessing its potential impact. We will take appropriate action as needed.”

Behind the scenes, when OpenSSH is built without PAM, it verifies passwords by hashing the password attempt using the C library’s crypt() function. It then compares the result to the account’s stored password field. So far, so good.

But when a key is longer than 256 bytes, musl’s crypt() function returns the string * as an error indicator. That is also what the password field holds for any account with no password, such as a key-only account. Thus, the “hash” matches and the login succeeds, and the attacker walks in.

Also: The new Cutefish Linux distro might be the best MacOS clone around

To exploit it, an attacker only needs the name of such an account. The attacker also needs at least one other account on the machine with a 5 or 6 password hash, and then to send a password of 257 to 1,024 bytes. According to Alpine, one user with a password is enough to make every passwordless account accessible.

In Linux systems that use glibc’s crypt(), which is almost all of them, the same attempt would return NULL on error, which sshd already rejects, so Debian, Ubuntu, Fedora, etc., aren’t affected.

According to *sshole, the group that uncovered the problem, this is a side effect that has been hiding since 2016.

Alpine said every release it checked is affected: 3.21 through 3.24, edge, and the older 3.16 to 3.20 branches. Exposure requires the following:

Root is not directly exposed in most cases. It has shipped as root:* in the base image since about Alpine 3.16, but OpenSSH’s default PermitRootLogin prohibit-password blocks it. Another account that can become root, for example through doas, still gives an attacker a route to root.

Alpine singled out its official tiny-cloud images, versions 3.18.11 through 3.24.3, as especially vulnerable. These ship with an alpine user with a * password field and passwordless doas. They are safe on first boot but become exploitable once any 6 hash exists on the machine. That can happen when an administrator sets a console password or when user data adds a user with a hashed password, which means, essentially, all of them.

Alpine released 3.21.10, 3.22.8, 3.23.8 and 3.24.4 on Thursday, with the OpenSSH fix among the security changes. It also published fixed packages for each release back to 3.16. Alpine 3.21 reached end of support on Nov. 1.

The just-released OpenSSH 10.6p2 avoids the problem in sshd. Alpine’s stable branches carry a patch for the same problem. You should run apk upgrade -U -a and then restart the daemon with rc-service sshd restart. Alpine stressed that upgrading the package does not replace the running daemon. Anyone who cannot update should set PasswordAuthentication no and restart sshd, after confirming that key login works.

Also: Maze Linux is the best security-focused distro I’ve ever tested

To check for an attack, Alpine told administrators to search /var/log/messages for “Accepted password for” lines that name an account with no password. It also advised reviewing dedicated OpenBSD application subexecutor (doas) use and recently created users, files, and authorized keys. “If you cannot rule out a login, treat the machine as compromised.” Just what you wanted for your weekend, I know, but here we are.

"I think it is kind of surprising that Wrexham have been so poor this season, so they may want to bring him back," Patrick told FLW.

"If Wrexham were having a decent season in the Championship, they wouldn't need him.

"There is a chance that they may want to buy someone else in the January transfer window and spend a bit of money, but there is a chance that they will want Keillor-Dunn back.

"I suppose we'll just have to wait and see on that one.

"Wrexham have been having a dreadful season, and with Josh Windass out as well, they may want to bring Keillor-Dunn back for some attacking prowess."

With Louie Barry out injured, Keillor-Dunn has been Wednesday's main attacking threat in recent weeks, so it would certainly be a major blow if they were unable to extend his loan beyond January.

Wrexham could opt to recall Keillor-Dunn in January after Smith's season-ending injury, but he has been deemed surplus to requirements for much of his time at the SToK Cae Ras, so it seems more likely that the Red Dragons will enter the market for a replacement.

Should Sheffield Wednesday try to extend Davis Keillor-Dunn's loan at Hillsborough in January? Tell us in the comments below!

While he did not directly confirm he wanted to remain at Hillsborough for the season, Keillor-Dunn revealed last month that he was loving his time in South Yorkshire and claimed that he was "sure" Wednesday would be in discussions with his parent club over a potential extension, so the Owls should be hopeful of retaining the forward.

The Business Platinum Card® from American Express
