As High As 175,000 points. Find Out Your Offer.

If you’re a frequent traveler, scoring elite status with a major carrier like Delta Air Lines can make your journeys more relaxing and rewarding.

However, deciding which airline loyalty program benefits you the most is difficult. This is mainly due to the variations between complimentary upgrades, bonus points or miles, lounge access and other valuable perks. Plus, is it even worth pursuing elite status?

This guide breaks down Delta’s SkyMiles Medallion program, including its four public elite status tiers, how to earn status in 2027 through both travel and non-travel expenses, and the benefits of each level.

Here is everything you need to know about elite Medallion status in the Delta SkyMiles program.

Delta awards elite status to qualifying SkyMiles members each year. Elite status offers a range of benefits — from seat selection to priority boarding — that make travel more comfortable and convenient.

The airline has four published tiers of elite status:

Delta also has an invite-only status tier called Delta 360. While the airline doesn't publish its standard qualification criteria, big spenders could get an invitation to this very special club. Delta also says, “Members that have reached the 5 Million Miler milestone will receive an annual complimentary invitation for Delta 360° Membership.” That's a pretty sweet perk.

Visit our Delta Medallion glossary page for more details.

Flyers earn Medallion status based on their eligible activity during the preceding calendar year. Delta tracks travelers' activity through Medallion Qualification Dollars, which they earn by flying with Delta and its partner airlines, spending on select Delta SkyMiles American Express cards and booking Delta Vacations packages.

For Delta-marketed flights operated by Delta or a partner airline, members generally earn 1 MQD for every dollar spent on the eligible ticket price (including the base fare and carrier-imposed surcharges but excluding government-imposed taxes and fees). For eligible partner-marketed flights, MQD earnings are generally based on a percentage of the distance flown and the purchased fare class, according to Delta's published partner earning charts.

Note: Delta Main Basic tickets generally don't earn MQDs or qualify for Medallion upgrades.

Related: Your guide to Delta Air Lines Million Miler status

You can also earn MQDs on eligible award tickets and through everyday spending on the Delta SkyMiles Platinum, Platinum Business, Reserve and Reserve Business American Express cards. All four cards offer an annual 2,500-MQD Headstart, while eligible spending earns 1 MQD per $20 on Platinum cards and 1 MQD per $10 on Reserve cards.

Your status is valid through Jan. 31 of the following year. For example, travelers who qualify in 2026 will earn 2027 Medallion status, which would be valid through Jan. 31, 2028.

You must meet the applicable Medallion Qualification Dollar thresholds each calendar year to maintain that status.

Delta used to have several ways to earn status, but now the carrier uses only MQDs as qualification criteria. Below are the requirements to obtain 2027 Medallion status:

Delta did not raise the status requirements for 2027 status.

Here are some key benefits of each of Delta’s status tiers.

This is the entry tier in Delta’s program, and you’ll receive benefits like:

Delta’s mid-tier status includes Silver Medallion benefits, plus:

In addition to the Gold Medallion perks, you’ll also get the following:

You’ll get everything detailed above, plus some even better perks:

Check Delta’s website to review the full list of benefits available to SkyMiles Medallion members.

To make it easier to earn elite status, Delta gives eligible frequent flyers who also hold the following cobranded cards a head start on MQD metrics with a boost of 2,500 MQDs (per eligible card type) toward status qualification at the start of the year (and each year thereafter, until further notice):

Since the MQD head start is awarded per eligible card type, you’ll enjoy more than one 2,500 MQD boost if you have multiple eligible cards. Additionally, you’ll be able to enjoy bonus MQDs based on everyday spending on these cards.

Specifically, the Delta SkyMiles Reserve Amex and the Delta SkyMiles Reserve Business Amex will accrue 1 MQD for every $10 spent. Meanwhile, the Delta SkyMiles Platinum Amex and the Delta SkyMiles Platinum Business Amex will accrue 1 MQD for every $20 spent.

Technically, you could earn Delta Medallion status without ever stepping on a plane, simply by putting enough eligible spending on a cobranded credit card. But that doesn't necessarily make it a good strategy,

Even with the MQD Headstart, reaching the higher status tiers through credit card spending alone requires a large financial commitment. Before putting everyday purchases on a Delta card to chase status, consider whether the rewards and benefits justify the cost, when there are lots of other travel credit cards that could offer better returns for you personally.

If you earned Delta elite status in 2025, it’s valid through Jan. 31, 2027. If you qualify in 2026, your status will last until Jan. 31, 2028.

It’s critical to consider how much you’ll be traveling in the future to decide if Medallion status is worth it. If you push hard to reach Diamond Medallion status, the valuable perks outlined above apply only when traveling.

With the high MQD requirements, you would need to spend a significant amount of money with the airline or on a cobranded card to earn this top-tier status. So, you should ensure you're traveling enough to enjoy it.

That said, if you wind up within striking distance of the next tier, consider whether the benefits are worth it for you personally. There’s no reason to go out of your way for perks that don’t matter to you. Pursuing elite status with an airline you can’t realistically fly regularly isn't a great move. Look at Delta’s offerings from your home airport and whether it would be feasible to rely on that airline.

This elite-status hobby has many trade-offs, and one of the most common is deciding whether to use your preferred airline or hotel chain when neither is the most convenient nor the cheapest. Would you book a one-stop Delta flight if Southwest Airlines had a cheaper, nonstop option? If the answer is no, it may not be worth going out of your way to earn Delta elite status (or any elite status, for that matter).

The final consideration involves travel rewards credit cards. Many airline cobranded cards offer perks that mirror what you’d enjoy as an elite member. For example, the includes a free checked bag worldwide and a second checked bag when flying domestic, priority boarding and a 20% inflight savings in the form of statement credit. The card has no annual fee for the first year, then a $150 annual fee (see rates and fees).

Meanwhile, when you fly with Delta, the Delta SkyMiles Reserve American Express Card provides 15 Sky Club lounge visits each year (Feb. 1 through Jan. 31). Cardmembers also qualify for various complimentary seat upgrades and receive an annual companion certificate (valid for round-trip domestic and short-haul international flights to Mexico, the Caribbean and Central America in economy, Delta Comfort, Delta Premium Select or first class). To earn unlimited Sky Club visits each year, the total eligible purchases on the card must equal $75,000 or more between Jan. 1 and Dec. 31 each calendar year.

If you can pay a flat annual fee for one of these cards and gain access to perks that matter to you without going out of your way to earn elite status, that may be a good plan.

Delta Medallion status can make flying more comfortable, particularly if you're a frequent Delta customer who can take advantage of all the perks. But the value of those perks really depends on how often you fly and how much you're willing to spend to earn them.

For most travelers, the goal shouldn't necessarily be "reach Diamond Medallion." Instead, focus on the status tier that offers benefits you'll actually use and avoid spending more or booking less convenient flights just to earn a higher level of status.

If you’re considering switching your loyalty, we hope this detailed review of Delta’s elite status program has helped you make your decision.

The Fan Traditions You Need to Know This NWSL Postseason

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The 2026 NWSL playoffs kick off on Friday, with single-elimination play that's guaranteed to be a nail-biter. Eight teams made the playoffs, but only one will hoist the championship trophy on November 21.

Of course, part of the fun of watching any playoff game is knowing insider-y details about each team. That includes fan traditions that make a team unique—and that you can join in on, too.

Every team has its own special chants and traditions that are uniquely theirs, and knowing what they are before kickoff immediately creates a level of familiarity you wouldn't have otherwise. You may already have "your" NWSL team that you root for, and you're probably familiar with their special traditions. But there are also likely teams you'll be watching during playoffs that you don't know the ins and outs of.

Whether you're looking for another club to root for when yours isn't playing or you just want to brush up on the details, these are the unique and sometimes hilarious fan traditions to know this NWSL postseason.

The San Diego Wave fans aren't messing around. They've formed their own supporters' group called the San Diego Sirens, and they're happy to include everyone in the club.

The Sirens have an online breakdown of songs and chants to know ahead of games. They even included helpful clips, so you can learn the melodies ahead of games. (There's a lot of water-themed content in there, making these very on-brand.)

The Utah Royals' fandom is called the Throne, and they march into the stadium as a united front before kickoff. (The Throne also says that everyone is welcome to join in.) The Royals also list their go-to chants online, allowing fans new and old to brush up before games.

Cloud 9 is Gotham's supporters club, and they've borrowed a few traditions from the UK's Manchester United. That includes incorporating John Denver’s “Take Me Home, Country Roads,” and making it “Gotham Roads,” as a tribute to the team.

You can download the club's songs on your phone and take them to the game or to your watch party to follow along.

The Royal Guard is Seattle Reign's fan club, and they've got plenty of traditions to share. The group does a walk toward Lumen Field before kickoff and incorporates a match-day band that might be the largest in NWSL. Check out their chants—with emojis to show when to clap—to get in the spirit.

The Kansas City Current has three different groups of supporters to make game day a high-energy event. That includes the KC Blue Crew, Surface Tension Drumming Club, and Undertow KC.

The organizations work together and have an online list of chants, the music they're set to, sheet music for drummers, and even videos to help fans get up to speed.

Not to be outdone, Angel City has six recognized supporter groups to cheer on its team—the most in the NWSL. Those groups regularly pack “La Fortaleza,” the supporters section. The groups share chants, and you can brush up on them online.

Rebellion 99 is Angel City's largest supporter group. Along with the other groups, they do chants and set off flares to keep the energy going. Rebellion 99 even has a scholarship program for local youth, stressing the importance of community-buildin

Washington Spirit offers four different fan clubs to choose from. One of the team's major groups, called the Spirit Squadron, marches to the stadium together before games. They also hilariously have a chant that they stole from the English Premier League called "Let’s pretend we scored a goal" when things aren't going so well.

There's also a stadium tradition: At the 51st minute, a "Free D.C." chant starts as a nod to the push for Washington, D.C., to become the 51st state.

The Portland Thorns' support groups are the Rose City Riveters and Timbers Army. They have a slew of chants you can download to follow along. In the mix: A chant called "Rose City 'Til I Die," which they do when the other team scores.

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

A Costa Rican Beach Resort with a World-Class Coffee Program

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In Hotels With Great Taste, we’re pulling back the curtain for a peek at the “special sauce” that hotels use to create memorable, meaningful culinary experiences for their guests.

Check-in at Nekajui on Costa Rica’s verdant Papagayo Peninsula happens inside a sparsely furnished bungalow the staff call La Casita. But walk across the drive, past a massive ceiba tree hung heavy with lanterns, and all humility vanishes. The whole property sweeps out before you: an endless green lawn, pools cascading down two levels, and, finally, the serenely sparkling bay beyond.

Nekajui is Ritz Carlton’s first Reserve resort in Central or South America. The Reserve’s are Ritz Carlton’s nod to the idea of complete escape, often in the far flung retreats—the Chinese Himalayas, or the Saudi Arabian desert.

Now, the recently opened Nekajui allows you to unwind on the lush Costa Rican coast teeming with wildlife like capuchins, coatis, and iguanas. Hidden away at the remote property, you’ll also find a local coffee program that’s on par with the best big city cafes in the world.

Sprawling over 1400 acres, Nekajui is a dreamy jungle beach resort that opened its doors on the secluded peninsula in February 2025.

Each of the 107 rooms and suites peeks out onto treetop and ocean sunset views. Inside, the decor features outdoor access and carved wood motifs typical of the Guanacaste region.

Many rooms also have access to patios large enough for morning yoga sessions and private plunge pools (take note: if a guest above you has one, you may see water splashing down your way). For larger parties, treetop tents and villas on the property afford even more space than standard rooms.

The main campus includes rooms, restaurants, and a spa, but the property’s grounds extend: a funicular (like a tiny cable car) runs dramatically down the cliffs, past howler monkeys, to the beach with water warm as a pleasant bath and a cave to explore.

Peruvian chef Diego Muñoz, whose career includes stints at world-renowned Spanish restaurants El Bulli and Mugaritz, leads the culinary program at the property’s main restaurants Puna and the tiny, six-seat chef’s counter within, Paruro.

At Puna and Parura, Muñoz’s menu is filled with dishes with the layered flavors and artistic compositions that helped push Peruvian cuisine into the spotlight on a global stage. Puna has a fusion feel , sliding flavors and spices from Munoz’s international travels into Peruvian classics like a robust sudado, and zippy ceviche. Paruro, the chefs counter located inside Puna, is an East Asian experience with bites like a nori-cured tenderloin and a seabass curry. You’ll find one of Munoz’s best creations, a deboned, prawn-stuffed chicken wing, on both menus.

It’s important to Muñoz that Puna uses local ingredients that are uncommon on Costa Rican menus. Fresh seafood, for example, can be a particular challenge to source, even as the overwhelming majority of Costa Rican territory is marine.

Aspirations are high for the beverage program as well. Cooper’s is a moody, mysterious cocktail space with a menu full of unexpected Guanacaste ingredients. It leans into the speakeasy style, and as such, is invite-only, but don’t be intimidated—schmooze your server a bit, or ask your personal concierge to score you an invite.

Inside, the menu is ambitious, dotted with some adventurous local distillates—goat cheese, cascara, mango leaf—which find their way into thrilling riffs on classics like the martini-esque Turrialba using fino sherry and the aforementioned goat cheese distillate.

Elsewhere on the property, you can dine at Niri Beach Club, with an Iberian-focused menu, and the all-day Cafe Rincon which has sandwiches, bowls, and pastries.

Nekajui’s commitment to on-the-ground ingredients shines brightest in its coffee program atCafé Rincon. Fabi Rojas operates the micro-roastery at Nekajui, which features always updating selections from each of Costa Rica’s seven coffee growing regions.

For a country about the size of West Virginia, Costa Rica produces an impressive diversity of coffee: easy-drinking crowd pleasers in the lower elevations of the coastal west, bright acidic pops from Valle Occidental in the north, fruity, prize-winning geishas—one of which fetched over $300 per pound at auction—in the mountainous Los Santos region of the south central. And Fabi has nurtured personal relationships with farmers all over the country to ensure she has access to all of it.

And while she’ll make your coffee however you want it, the move is to have it brewed in a vandola. The one piece clay carafe and pour-over cone looks like something that might date back hundreds of years, but the design actually came from Costa Rican coffee roaster and barista Minor Alfaro in 2015. Brewing in clay helps maintain aroma longer, and the valve into the base of the dripper allows more gentle oxygenation according to Alfaro. Oxygen is usually the enemy of coffee, but allowing a small amount in through the valve can sweeten the flavor.

Besides brewing coffee better than my Chemex, each vandola is a handmade work of ceramic art—an apt vessel to showcase coffees grown, roasted, and brewed with exquisite care.

The Mysterious Fridge Hacks Hitting Grocery Stores

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Welcome to Open Tab, a weekly column covering the news, gossip, and stories that have stayed open in my tabs all week. Last time we covered the Cheesecake Factory culture war.

Death, taxes, and Martha Stewart doing something weird online every six to eight weeks: These are the constants in our ever-chaotic lives. This round involved a quickly deleted Instagram post showing Stewart apparently dressed as a homeless person—she was holding a clipboard (?) with a message in marker that read “I am hungry help me!!”

She released a series of statements apologizing for the gaffe, and at one point seemed to blame Today show host Craig Melvin, who, she said, spilled chocolate on the sweater she’d planned on wearing to a costume party later that night, forcing her to improvise a new costume on the fly.

But enough of Martha! We have more important things to worry about.

Also in today’s Open Tab: the mysterious refrigeration system hacks plaguing Europe, DC's newly minted foie gras ban, the GLP-1 gap relationship, and a democratic victory is celebrated at a Whataburger, naturally.

Dozens of supermarkets across seven countries were the victims of hacking that took control of their refrigeration systems this week. The hackers shifted grocery freezers into defrost mode, the freezers began blowing hot air onto the frozen food, and, in some, temperatures rose to 122 degrees. Needless to say a lot of food was deemed spoiled and had to be thrown out.

Three weeks ago, similar attacks hit grocery stores in France. It was the same story: defrost mode, hot air, lots of wasted food and money. Officials are investigating who may be responsible, but so far the culprits and their motives remain a mystery. The BBC reports that experts believe hackers in Russia or Iran—or simply teenagers playing a prank—could be responsible.

Could something like this happen in the US? Well, it sort of already has. In August six military bases across the country reported some odd refrigeration malfunctions—the same kind we’ve been seeing in Europe. The Pentagon is investigating the incident as a suspected hack, but that’s about as much information as we can glean.

The FBI on Thursday issued a warning about “malicious cyber activity targeting industrial refrigeration and cold-chain monitoring systems around the world.”

As Democrats celebrated a resounding wave of victories in this week’s midterm elections, so too did duck and goose lovers. Washington, DC, voted to approve a ballot measure banning the sale and production of foie gras. It was a controversial issue in the area, as I covered in last week’s Open Tab video, which saw big name chefs come forward to defend the making, selling, and eating of foie gras. The discourse got heated, topical Halloween costumes abounded.

The ban will go into effect July 1, 2027, so foie-heads have some time to get their fill. But it’s not that simple. A New York foie gras company has said it will take legal action against the ban, calling it “unconstitutional on several grounds." To put the ban in effect would face further logistical barriers too. DC’s chief financial officer has estimated it would cost more than $300,000 over four fiscal years to implement it—money that’s not currently allocated in the budget.

Despite the upcoming ban, Bart Hutchins, chef and co-owner of Butterworth’s who was one of the ban’s most vocal opponents, has said he’ll continue serving foie gras in his restaurants. “I will still be serving it for another decade,” he told the Washington Post.

What happens when one partner gets on a GLP-1 and the other partner doesn’t? We know that GLP-1’s can shift your sex drive, and that they can have an effect on your mental health, but often, it seems, there’s anecdotal evidence that they can have a profound effect on your relationships. A report in the Washington Post details how, for some couples, starting a GLP-1 can disrupt a couple's routines and equilibrium—deciding on a date night restaurant, for instance—which can create divisions. There simply isn’t enough data to measure how GLP-1’s might be linked to divorce rates, but some experts point to statistics attached to bariatric surgeries, another weight loss intervention, which found that married couples are twice as likely to get divorced after getting the procedure.

Midterm elections in the US brought a number of historic firsts, including the first Muslim senator (Michigan), the first openly gay man senator (New Hampshire), and the first Black woman governor (Georgia). Notably, voters managed to flip Texas, turning the state blue for the first time in decades.

How did Texas Senator-elect James Talarico and Governor-elect Gina Hinojosa celebrate their wins? At a Whataburger. It’s a surprisingly poignant statement about the affordability politics that was foundational to many Democratic wins this election cycle. Read on to learn more.

Your Business May Be Invisible to AI. Here Are 7 Simple Fixes You Can Make This Week.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Learn how to improve AI visibility with seven practical website, content and credibility fixes that help customers discover and evaluate your business.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

Imagine a prospect asking an AI assistant to recommend a business in your category. Three competitors appear. Yours does not. You have a functioning website, years of experience and a steady stream of content. Yet the customer never learns you exist.

That scenario is worth investigating, but not because AI has a single secret ranking formula. Different tools generate different responses. Some retrieve live web pages; others depend on a combination of search results and existing knowledge. A recommendation can change with the question, location, timing and context. One test is not proof that your business is universally invisible.

The real opportunity is simpler. Make it easier for a person, a search engine and an AI-powered discovery tool to understand exactly what you offer, who benefits and what evidence supports your claims. You can begin with seven manageable improvements, many of which strengthen traditional search and conversions at the same time.

Start with customer language, not marketing jargon. If you sell cybersecurity services to banks, someone may ask, “Who helps regional banks prepare for a security audit?” They are less likely to type your carefully crafted positioning statement. Your first job is to understand the questions that occur before a buying decision.

Write five to 10 realistic prompts from sales calls, customer emails and proposal requests. Include a category question, a problem question, a comparison question and one that names your market. Test them in more than one AI search experience. Record the date, precise wording, companies mentioned, cited sources and whether your own website is among those sources.

Keep branded prompts separate. “Tell me about Acme Consulting” checks brand recognition; “Which firms can improve manufacturing supply-chain forecasting?” investigates discovery by an unfamiliar buyer. Confusing the two creates false confidence.

Your 20-minute action: Create a spreadsheet with columns for prompt, platform, date, cited source, competitor and opportunity. Treat observations as directional research, not an audited visibility score. Repeat the same prompt set monthly to look for persistent patterns.

Too many homepages use language that could describe any company: “We unlock transformational growth through next-generation solutions.” It sounds sophisticated but leaves a buyer wondering what is actually for sale. If a customer cannot understand the offer in ten seconds, that is a messaging problem long before it becomes a technical SEO problem.

Replace abstraction with a simple structure: “We help [specific audience] solve [specific problem] through [specific product or service], so they can [realistic outcome].” For example: “We help multi-location clinics improve patient acquisition and appointment conversion through search, content and website optimization.”

Then add proof nearby: industries served, a concrete capability, a customer example or a documented credential. Do not paste an entire keyword list into your opening paragraph. Make the first 100 words accurate, readable and specific, with one primary H1 that matches the subject of the page.

Your 20-minute action: Ask someone outside your organization to read the first screen of your homepage. Can they explain whom you serve, what you deliver and what action to take next? If not, rewrite it before investing in additional content.

Businesses often publish broad thought leadership but hide the details a buyer needs: price ranges, implementation steps, eligibility, limitations and alternatives. That leaves both customers and answer-generating systems with incomplete information.

Ask sales and customer-service teams which questions recur immediately before someone books a meeting or requests a quote. Select five. Build accurate answers into the relevant service or product pages, not a disconnected wall of generic FAQs. Use a descriptive H2 question and answer it directly in the first sentence, followed by examples, constraints and the next decision a buyer needs to make.

If pricing varies, explain the variables rather than inventing a price. If delivery timelines depend on approvals, say so. If the product is not suitable for every customer, acknowledge that explicitly. Useful answers increase confidence because they reduce uncertainty, not because they repeat a search phrase.

This approach aligns with a broader shift toward commercially useful content. For additional perspective, Entrepreneur has covered how businesses can earn leads across both AI search and conventional Google discovery.

Your 45-minute action: Publish one complete answer on each of your top five revenue-driving pages. Review the result on mobile. Does a buyer get the information without opening an accordion, guessing terminology or scheduling a sales call?

“Trusted,” “innovative” and “industry-leading” are not proof. Any competitor can make the same claims. Instead, help a skeptical prospect verify what you say. That might mean a named subject-matter expert, clear methodology, customer references with consent, original observations, dated research, documented product features or a candid case study.

An effective case study explains the initial problem, what changed, how success was measured and what the outcome means. A claimed 300% improvement without a baseline is less persuasive than a transparent account of the metric, time period and relevant limitations. Not every business can disclose client names, but permissioned or anonymized evidence can still be useful if presented accurately.

For expertise-led services, include author credentials, an updated bio and an explanation of who is responsible for the advice. For regulated industries, prioritize accurate, appropriately reviewed content over speed.

Your 30-minute action: Select your most profitable service page and add one piece of verifiable evidence plus a short explanation of why it matters to the buyer. Then identify what proof is still missing.

You can write a perfect page and undermine it with a noindex directive, a broken canonical tag, restrictive robots rules or weak internal linking. Start by checking the pages that generate or could generate revenue. Avoid treating technical optimization as a giant sitewide project when a focused inspection of five pages will reveal practical priorities.

Use Google Search Console to inspect indexing and important search queries. Confirm that key page content is present as readable text, accessible from relevant navigation and consistent with its title and meta description. Structured data can support eligible search features when it accurately represents visible content, but no special schema guarantees inclusion in AI answers.

Google’s official guidance for generative AI search reinforces that strong SEO foundations and distinctive, useful content remain central. There is no requirement to purchase a special AI markup package.

If you want public pages eligible for inclusion in ChatGPT Search, examine bot-access policies too. OpenAI distinguishes its search crawler from other crawlers, so site owners should review the current documentation rather than assuming all AI bot traffic serves the same purpose.

Your 30-minute action: Inspect five commercially important URLs for index eligibility, broken links, visible text and relevant internal links. Assign each issue to a named owner with a completion date.

A website is necessary, but it is also a controlled channel. Buyers often validate a company through independent sources, including industry associations, credible media, customer reviews, professional directories and expert participation. Those sources are valuable because they can corroborate facts, not because a business purchased a large volume of mentions.

Start with accuracy: Is your business name consistent? Are leaders and services described correctly? Are your credentials up to date? Can a customer find substantive contributions rather than a directory entry alone? Then focus on places that matter in your market. A healthcare founder may benefit more from a recognized professional association or reputable sector publication than a generic syndication network.

Entrepreneur has also examined the role of credible outside recognition in a changing search environment. The practical lesson is to earn citations and mentions through work worth referencing rather than mechanically distributing the same press release everywhere.

Your 45-minute action: Correct three important third-party profiles, then identify one legitimate opportunity to contribute an original data point, expert explanation or customer insight to an industry publication.

It is tempting to turn AI visibility into a single number. But being named frequently does not guarantee revenue, and AI answers are inconsistent enough that screenshots are a weak stand-alone measurement system. Separate the signals you can observe from the outcomes your organization actually needs.

Track whether your brand appears for a consistent set of buyer questions. Record citations where platforms expose them. Review identifiable AI referral traffic in analytics, but recognize that some journeys will not carry an obvious referral label. Combine those indicators with form submissions, qualified meetings, pipeline and revenue. Ask new prospects how they discovered you, while understanding that self-reported attribution is imperfect.

For leadership reporting, a simple monthly scorecard beats a glossy dashboard that cannot explain its methodology. Show changes to priority pages, visible search and referral trends, sales opportunities and what you plan to test next. Make it clear which trends are observed, estimated or unavailable.

Your 30-minute action: Establish a baseline this month and choose one commercial metric to improve. A rise in citations may be encouraging; a rise in qualified opportunities is the outcome the business can use.

Do not confuse a seven-day implementation sprint with seven-day rankings. Search systems can take time to discover and process changes. Results depend on competition, platform behavior, website quality and the strength of your evidence. The point of the sprint is to eliminate obvious weaknesses and create a defensible plan.

AI search has changed the way some buyers research providers, but it has not removed the need for fundamentals. A clear offer, useful answers, accessible pages, credible evidence and disciplined measurement improve the experience for the human buyer first. They also give discovery systems more accurate material to work with.

The question for business owners is not, “How do I trick AI into recommending me?” It is, “Have I given customers and credible sources enough useful information to understand why my company belongs on the shortlist?” That is a much more productive place to begin.

For organizations that want to examine these issues more systematically, I use an AI visibility assessment approach through Bullzeye Media Marketing’s GEO practice. The same starting principle applies whether you work with an advisor or manage it internally: Diagnose the gaps before spending on execution.

Imagine a prospect asking an AI assistant to recommend a business in your category. Three competitors appear. Yours does not. You have a functioning website, years of experience and a steady stream of content. Yet the customer never learns you exist.

That scenario is worth investigating, but not because AI has a single secret ranking formula. Different tools generate different responses. Some retrieve live web pages; others depend on a combination of search results and existing knowledge. A recommendation can change with the question, location, timing and context. One test is not proof that your business is universally invisible.

The real opportunity is simpler. Make it easier for a person, a search engine and an AI-powered discovery tool to understand exactly what you offer, who benefits and what evidence supports your claims. You can begin with seven manageable improvements, many of which strengthen traditional search and conversions at the same time.

Project Hail Mary Author Andy Weir Knows How To Get Star Trek Back On Track [Exclusive]

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Andy Weir, the author of the hit novels "The Martian" and "Project Hail Mary," has seen the state of modern "Star Trek" and feels he knows how to fix it. In brief, he wants to get back to basics. In a new interview with /Film's Jeremy Mathai, Weir said he feels that the franchise should focus on making a TV series — not a movie — about a Starfleet vessel on a simple mission of exploration into deep space. No bells and whistles, no gimmicks. Just plain, baseline "Trek."

What does Weir mean by getting "Star Trek" back on track? Well, the franchise has experienced a lot of ups and downs throughout its 60-year history, and we're at a point where, many might agree, "Star Trek" became a little too ambitious for its own good. Starting in 2017, "Star Trek" churned out eight (!) original shows (nine, if you count "Very Short Treks"), and one TV movie. The two current Trek shows — "Strange New Worlds" and "Starfleet Academy" — will run their course next year. There are currently no new "Star Trek" shows in active production.

The franchise had an excellent run from 1987 through 2005 (the beginning of "Star Trek: The Next Generation" through the end of "Star Trek: Enterprise"), but by the end of that era, the saga had kind of petered out, and audiences had lost interest. "Star Trek" experienced a resurgence in 2009 with J.J. Abrams' reboot movie, and that carried through two hit sequels. That era, however, could never get its feet underneath it for a fourth movie, and that, too, petered out. Now the Paramount+ era is petering out as well.

Andy Weir thinks a single show about exploration might be the way forward.

In his interview with /Film, Andy Weir rightfully pointed out that "Star Trek" has always functioned best when it is a weekly, episodic TV series about varied adventures throughout the galaxy. Weir feels that making a new movie at this point would be unwise. He also notes that this proposed new series wouldn't have to rely on nostalgia, existing characters, or the USS Enterprise. To quote:

"[I]f I was the boss of 'Trek' [...], I would try to establish ... You've got to make a show, a series. I personally wouldn't try to go straight to films. I would go back to a series, a streaming show maybe that's just called 'Star Trek,' and then work from there. It doesn't have to be about the Enterprise. 'Star Trek' is a rich genre that has lots and lots of cool stuff in it. It could have that same feel. It could be about a different ship exploring strange new worlds and new civilizations."

Weir pointed out that "Star Trek" follows a specific DNA, which is, in his words, "this is a spaceship that goes out, finds new things, and has adventures." For him, "Star Trek" shouldn't aim higher than that, especially not after a decade of streaming shows that reached in all directions at once. The Paramount+ era has seen many shows taking place across the timeline of "Star Trek," with some being episodic and some being serialized. Some contain R-rated levels of violence, while others are aimed at toddlers. It's all too much.

Of course, the idea of a "Star Trek" series about a single starship, not the Enterprise, going on weekly adventures in deep space was the premise of 1995's "Star Trek: Voyager." Weir feels, though, that "Voyager" was not exactly what he means. He also thought that its immediate predecessor, "Star Trek: Deep Space Nine," while amazing, wasn't quite the "Star Trek" of a generation previous. Weir said that the original 1966 "Star Trek" made use of the franchise's DNA, as did "Next Generation," but after that, each show became a departure from the desired norm. He said:

"'Voyager' departed from it a little too much. People didn't like it. 'Deep Space Nine' had its own completely cool story. It was more of a political thriller, and they got away with it, but they didn't try to be 'Star Trek' with a slight twist. They're like, 'We're "Babylon 5." We're doing our own thing. We're doing it in the Star Trek universe, but we're doing our own thing.' So that's what I would do with Star Trek, is go back to the basics, go back to a ship exploring the cosmos."

"Babylon 5" is another sci-fi series, contemporaneous with "Deep Space Nine," that was also set on board a space station and that involved a lot of political intrigue. /Film loves "Deep Space Nine," but some people, like Weir, argue that it wasn't quite true to the Trek spirit. There wasn't enough trekking for his taste, one might suppose. But if the franchise did some simple star trekking, Weir would be happy.

With a new film in development, the folks in charge seem to be taking a different approach.

Apple Won't Make a Small Phone so This Company Is Stepping In

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Big smartphones have long been the norm. It seems many, if not most, of us prefer our smartphones to be akin to tiny tablets. Even Apple's smallest iPhone isn't that small, with a 6.1-inch display. (Compare that to the 5.4-inch iPhone mini it used to sell.) But just because a lot of us like it that way doesn't mean all of us do.

Despite what companies like Apple would lead you to believe, there are plenty of people out there who want their smartphones to be smaller. For some, the iPhone mini was perfect, featuring a design that most could use comfortably with one hand. While the sales might not have been steady enough for Apple to continue producing the mini line, anecdotally, I know quite a few people who would happily keep using iPhones this small, if only Apple would make more of them. Perhaps you know people like this too; maybe you're one of them.

While the rumor mill does not suggest that Apple plans to make a new iPhone mini, that doesn't mean the dream of the tiny smartphone is dead. Enter "Enough Phone," a new device with a humble goal: to create a "small, modern smartphone that's enough."

Enough Phone really does seem small. The company says its upcoming device is 133mm by 64mm, and less than 12mm thick. That's the equivalent of 5.24 inches by 2.52 inches, and less than 0.47 inches thick. It's remarkably similar to the iPhone 13 mini, at 5.18 by 2.53 inches, and 0.3 inches thick. While the Enough Phone might be a tad larger overall, and perhaps noticeably thicker, it's still reminiscent of that "mini" footprint.

The Enough Phone's 5.3-inch display isn't particularly modern, however. While we don't know the display type, we do know that it has a resolution of 1520 x 720, which may look less sharp to anyone coming from the 2340 x 1080 resolution of the iPhone 13 mini. I'd wager that most people interested in a device like this aren't looking for an ultra-sharp OLED display anyway, but it's worth noting here.

As for the Enough Phone's other specs, the company says it plans to pack in a 2.4GHz MediaTek Dimensity 7500 chip with 8GB of RAM, 128GB of built-in storage, though you can expand that with a microSD card (something you don't see on many smartphones these days). The current plan is to ship with a single 50MP Sony IMX766 rear camera, and a 32MP selfie cam, which would be amongst the highest-resolution front-facing cameras on any smartphone. It supports 5G, Wi-Fi 6, and eSIM, with the option to use a physical SIM as well.

Enough is also aiming to make its smartphone more repairable than the competition. While smartphone OEMs have made slight improvements to repairability in recent years, few phones are made to be opened up by the user, and their components replaced. While Enough Phone isn't fully modular, it is designed with easy-to-open screws, and comes with a fully replaceable 3,500 mAh battery. Combined with promised software support through 2032, Enough says this device "should last you into the next decade."

Now, all of these specs are targets, as the phone is currently in the prototype stage. Enough says that final details may change "slightly" during testing. I would at least hope that the dimensions, particularly the screen size, remain the same by launch. What will likely stay the same, however, are the ringtones: Enough says they hired composer Brian Squillance of LAANDS to produce the jingles for its phone, which is a neat touch.

Unfortunately for Apple fans, however, Enough Phone isn't an iPhone. That said, it doesn't have to be an Android phone, either. While you can buy the phone with Android 17 installed, you can also choose to run OdysseyOS, a minimalist "de-Googled" operating system made by SLEKE. Enough says OdysseyOS is meant for "intentional phone use," which might appeal to anyone looking for a phone that's "dumb" as well as small.

Of course, neither operating system is iOS. Unfortunately for Apple users, the only true iPhone options here are to continue using an old iPhone mini, which Apple does still support, or to opt for an iPhone 17e, the smallest iPhone the company currently makes. It's pretty easy to find a used and refurbished iPhone 13 mini for reasonable price; but while they can still run the latest version of iOS, you won't be able to take advantage of all of Apple's newest features. Plus, while there's no telling how long Apple plans to support the 13 line for, it won't support it forever—by iOS 28 or iOS 29, your phone might lose support entirely.

If the promise of a modern mini smartphone entices you, you can back the Enough Phone on Kickstarter today. While the company's $399 "Super Early Bird" reward is already sold out, as of this article, the "Early Bird" reward is still up. That reward costs $449, and includes a glass screen protector pre-installed, a protective case, a USB-C cable, a USB-C to 3.5mm headphone adapter, a hex screwdriver, and green "Founders' Edition" backplate screws. Enough says that the retail price for its phone will be $599 after the campaign, so you can save $150 by backing the phone today—if you're serious about small phones. The Enough Phone is slated for a May 2027 launch.

AC Milan 0-2 Ferencvaros: Five things we learned – a poor squad picked apart

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

AC Milan’s Europa League misery continued at San Siro on Thursday night as they were beaten 2-0 by Ferencvaros.

Ruben Amorim made six changes to the team that won the derby against Inter, hoping that the entire group would remain buoyed by that performance to secure a much-needed win in Europe. However, what followed was the worst performance of his tenure so far.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Lenny Joseph netted twice in the second half to capitalise on errors and fire the Hungarian side to a memorable win. Ferencvaros were not even rocked by a red card to the substitute Yusuf as they kept the Rossoneri at bay quite easily, securing a two-goal win they deserved.

The result means that Milan have now dropped out of even the top 24 spots, and they can just about say goodbye to any hopes of direct last-16 qualification barring a miracle. Ivan Stoev has five things we learned from another dismal midweek game.

It’s difficult to pick five talking points after a game like this, as we can probably spend quite a bit of time discussing how bad each of the players were. But let’s try to address the bigger issue.

This squad is full of sheep, lacking both leaders and the quality to compete. We can talk all about how much Amorim rotates the squad and try to ease the blow by suggesting the Rossoneri didn’t field their best XI. Yet, if the management were serious about making the club competitive, then the rotational players should be above the quality of almost all teams competing in the Europa League, bar a few.

Obviously, if we assume Amorim is the right choice and Gerry Cardinale is serious, then it will take a lot more than a single transfer window and three new players to get Milan to compete on a few fronts, which was further proven against Ferencvaros.

Out of the whole squad, the only player that can change a game is Diego Moreira, who doesn’t play every game and has lots of room for improvement still. Not to mention that playing with the likes of Filippo Terracciano, Ardon Jashari and even Goncalo Ramos is not helping Belgium at all.

The rest of the squad lack either the quality or the mentality. The collective arrogance on the pitch was insufferable to watch, with Ferencvaros looking like the only side that had any desire to actually win. Whilst the Diavolo looked like they were playing a game in the park with no aggression, no focus, no intensity. No nothing.

It’s a bit pointless to discuss individuals because I think the wider point is clear – Milan lack leaders, Milan lack quality and Milan lack a game changer that can get the ball and make something happen when the tough gets going.

It was made evident after four games in the Europa League and a few blunders in Serie A that there’s lots of work to be done in January and next summer.

Again, it’s difficult to pick and choose which player to discuss, but given Ramos is the Rossoneri’s most expensive signing ever, it’s probably fair that the pressure starts mounting on him.

Listen, I wasn’t too bothered by the lack of goals initially, as the striker did well in the build-up and opened up space for his teammates; he also ran a lot in both phases of the game. Let’s also be honest, he has lacked the service to actually get the goal-scoring opportunities.

Nevertheless, in the last few games and especially against Ferencvaros, he has been dire – his build-up is poor, his ball control is pretty bad and when he does get a shot off, it’s usually miles away.

Now, obviously he’s not a striker that’s going to create chances for himself; he lacks the pace and the trickery, which is fine if you are good in the other aspects of the game. However, we can’t keep grasping for straws.

The reality is that the former PSG man is just not living up to the expectations given the hefty sum paid for him in the summer.

In retrospect, maybe it wasn’t wise to spend such a sum on a player who needs quality around him in order to be a better facilitator or to receive more chances overall. The reality as of now is that the Portuguese is a disappointment.

Again, as we said, most players in the squad lack quality or mentality and unfortunately for us there are some that lack both and it’s way past the tipping point at this stage.

Terracciano, for starters, has not shown anything worth mentioning in all of his games with the red and black shirt and there’s really no reason for him to get minutes. Even without pressure, he’s just not of the needed level.

Jashari is another one that the fans and the management have hoped will bounce back after his lengthy injury after joining Milan. Yet, he’s probably had one good game from start to finish and there’s always gaps in his game.

It’s probably wise at this point to accept he’s not going to live up to the expectations and a solution needs to be found on the market, especially given Luka Modric will most likely be retiring at the end of the season.

There are also other players in this category that didn’t necessarily participate in last night’s game but should have the same fate – Pervis Estupinan and Ruben Loftus-Cheek to name a couple.

If nothing else, these poor performances should highlight to the management where the gap in quality is and what the strategy on the transfer market should be.

4. De Winter’s disastrous performance proves costly

I think the biggest individual call-out should definitely be the Belgian, who played an awful back pass to Lorenzo Torriani on the first goal. Yes, the keeper could’ve and should’ve done much better, but that back pass was just so sloppy – it could put an outfield player in a tricky situation, let alone a goalkeeper that perhaps isn’t as comfortable with his feet.

This especially coming from a player who should’ve been a certainty at the back given the heavily rotated squad.

We can also partly criticise the intervention on the second goal, because it started the whole sequence leading to the Joseph’s brace. Yes, it was a bit unlucky where the ball ended up, but you can also argue it was sloppy, the same way you can argue that a keeper clearing the ball into the opponent’s feet after a shot instead of towards the corner is the keeper’s fault to an extent.

It’s just unacceptable to go from bullying Thuram to being bullied by not even Ferencvaros’s first-choice striker.

5. Amorim’s tactics fail, but the players also struggle

I think it’s probably fair to criticise the Portuguese coach a bit too, as the Hungarians totally outplayed the Diavolo at the San Siro and were tactically better. All in all, they had the answer to everything Milan tried to offer on the night.

At the same time, I’m torn to put the blame fully on Ruben Amorim because he did make a triple change at half-time, bringing on Pulisic, Moreira and Gila, which was still at 0-0. At that point, the Rossoneri had more than enough quality on the pitch to be able to deal with whatever Ferencvaros had to offer.

Instead, two individual mistakes gave away the opening goal and it all went downhill from there. Even after the red card, Milan struggled to create a big chance.

I personally struggle to blame this fully on tactics given all the circumstances and we are back to the point that the Milan squad are just not good enough and lacks that killer mentality.

Why I’d Rather Take a Buck in the Timber

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

IT WAS LIKE the buck fell from the sky. I’d glassed the wooded creekbottom to my left only minutes before. Then I scanned the opposite timber, stared at my boots for a few seconds, and heard the slightest scuff in the leaves. Or I thought I did. This was my third straight morning in the stand, the point at which I can begin to hear phantom, wishful-thinking noises. But when I turned to look in the direction of the sound, there stood the buck, just 25 yards off, his hooves planted in a scrape.

I wasn’t nervous at first. In profile, his tines looked OK but not towering, and his ears were hiding whatever mass was there. But then he arched his neck to reach for the licking branch, and I caught my breath. Suddenly I could see his jumble of stickers and wrist-thick bases. I reached for my bow while he worked the scrape. Then, backing off the sign, he stood at the junction of two trails, where I could see the full depth of his chest and size of his flank. Either trail would bring him to a shooting lane 20 yards off my setup. He chose the one on the right, and when I touched off the shot, he made a 30-yard sprint, stopped, and tipped over dead.

The buck’s rack was in my top five at the time, and his body still ranks as my biggest. But what sticks with me most about that Kansas buck is how the hunt went down. I’d walked into an unknown piece of timber, walked it for 30 minutes, analyzing rubs, scrapes, trails, and topography, and then finally picked a stand tree, thinking, This is the spot where I can kill a nice buck. That doesn’t always work, of course, but when it does, it’s about as close to the perfect deer-hunting experience as I can imagine.

That may seem a little strange in today’s food-plot era—a time when more and more hunters hope to ambush deer out in the open and when we pore over which seeds to sow and even over the “architecture” and “design” of our elaborate ambushes. Don’t get me wrong. I’m a dedicated food-plotter. In 2009, I arrowed the biggest buck of my life, a heavy-beamed giant that grossed more than 180 inches, in a plot I’d helped plant just a month prior. Every year, I put in plots to hold deer through the season, to keep them fed in winter, for better shed hunting in spring, and for trail-cam monitoring in summer. But when it comes to actually tagging a buck, I would much rather do it in the timber.

I think the reason is simple but often overlooked these days. A buck feeding in a food plot is plain to see and a sight to behold—but still just a buck in a food plot. There’s not much to figure out there. But a buck bedded in the timber or side-hilling along a faint trail on the far side of a wooded ridge is a mystery. If you want to see this buck, you have to solve a puzzle, and to me, that’s the real fun of deer hunting.

Related: Want more whitetail stories? Sign up for the free Whitetail 365 Newsletter here.

It’s also the challenge. Anyone can plant turnips in the hope of luring deer to a chosen spot. The best hunters I know can walk the deer’s turf—the places where they hide and feel safe and call home—and, after a little snooping around, tell you exactly where you can kill a monster. Years back, I was scouting with my buddy Bob, who has a wall full of whopper bucks that speak to his deep understanding of mature deer. I was covering ground as quickly and thoroughly as my legs would allow. But nearly every time I looked at Bob, he was just standing there, scanning the terrain. Finally Bob gestured to a slight crease on an otherwise steep hillside. “What’s up there?” he asked.

“Well, it’s like a mini-bench, but I’ve walked it a bunch and never found a scrape or rub,” I told him.

“Doesn’t matter,” Bob said. “When I scout a place like this, the first thing I do is just stop and look. Then I imagine I’m pouring a big bucket of water on a hillside like that. And whatever contours the water would follow, that’s where the bucks will walk. If the sign is there, fine. But you don’t need it to know. You can kill a buck on that little bench, guaranteed.”

That day completely changed how I scout for deer. I’m excited to find a thigh-thick rub or a scrape that looks like an elk wallow, but mostly I study terrain. It’s a skill that I think can never be completely mastered, but occasionally I am able to decipher why a buck will move through this funnel and not that one, or how three ravines separated by hundreds of yards will work together to make one fence crossing the most incredible stand site ever.

Which brings me to the other simple reason to love hunting the timber. When a buck shows up at your food plot, it’s usually somewhat expected. After all, you’ve set the whole thing up for that purpose and probably have trail-cam pics or have glassed him there before. But when a buck shows up under your stand in the timber, it’s almost always a surprise.

Related: Red Oak vs White Oak, a Complete Guide for Hunters

A few Novembers back, while F&S executive editor Dave Hurteau and I readied for a morning hunt here in Minnesota, I told him I was going to try a hang-and-hunt in a new spot. “I’ve just got this place in my head,” I said. “I scouted it last spring, and there was almost zero buck sign. But it felt right.”

I’d picked the tree during my spring scouting mission, and I somehow found it in the dark that morning. Three climbing sticks and a tree step had me at stand height, and I hung the set and settled in. The first 90 minutes after a November sunrise are typically magic, but I wasn’t surprised when I saw exactly zero deer. The reason the small oak hollow had stuck with me was that it funneled toward bedding cover immediately behind me. But the deer heading there were starting from corn and soybean and alfalfa fields on farms over a half mile away. It was going to take some time for them to reach me.

Still, when I looked at my watch at 10:25, I was starting to doubt my hunch. Four deerless hours at the peak of the rut typically add up to Sorry bud, you guessed wrong today. But then I looked up and saw this perfect 10-point rack weaving through the timber. He stopped at just 10 yards before my arrow slipped through him. He wasn’t one of my biggest or heaviest, but I’d never seen the buck before, on camera or otherwise, which made him a perfect surprise and a puzzle solved, as unexpectedly as a Kansas deer falling from the sky.

Trained as a high school English teacher and coach, Scott Bestul left that lucrative field to pursue another—as a full-time freelance writer in 1990. He sold his first story to Field & Stream a few years later and has been contributing to the brand ever since. Highlights Education Bestul graduated from Winona State University in 1985, with a B.S. in English (writing emphasis) and a coaching certificate. Experience Bestul has been the F&S whitetails columnist for more than a decade and has written dozens of articles and contributed to many feature packages since joining the brand. Additionally, he’s contributed to many national and regional outdoor magazines and newspapers. Bestul paired with F&S Executive Editor, Dave Hurteau, to write the The Total Deer Hunter Manual and The Total Bowhunting Manual. Bestul also co-authored The Last River Rat with Wisconsin resident Kenny Salwey in 2001. He was awarded the Quality Deer Management Association’s “Signpost Communicator of the Year” in 2013, and has given seminars at deer expos and sportsman’s shows across the country. Bestul will enjoy his 50th deer season this fall, and has been turkey hunting since the 1980s. He’s been an avid upland hunter and fisherman since boyhood. Gnosis Pay data breach exposes customer info; funds unaffected

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach after unauthorized access to its legacy debit card system on Nov. 3. Funds were unaffected; 3D Secure is now mandatory.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach affecting its legacy system powering Gnosis Pay-branded debit cards, exposing certain customer information after unauthorized access was detected on Nov. 3.

The company said it contained the incident within two hours, replaced compromised security credentials and addressed the vulnerability, adding that customer funds were not affected and its enterprise platform powering white-label card programs was untouched.

Gnosis Pay has made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online card payments until the affected system is retired on Dec. 20.

Crypto card providers increasingly rely on third-party infrastructure and legacy systems that can create security vulnerabilities. In recent months, there have been breaches exposing cardholder information and exploits affecting funds held in card accounts.

Based said an attacker accessed an internal dashboard used to manage its Visa card program on Oct. 5. Some customer data was exposed, but the attacker reportedly failed to access deposited funds. In August, a vulnerability in Rain's Solana card infrastructure allowed unauthorized withdrawals from card balances belonging to users of Tria and Avici.

Meanwhile, Revolut said an unauthorized third party used a fraudulent request from a legitimate government email domain to obtain sensitive customer information, potentially including account statements, identity documents and Bitcoin transaction histories. Customer funds and systems were unaffected.

Microsoft is winding down its deal to rent AI computing from Oracle

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The rented servers ran older Nvidia chips, and Microsoft is rethinking billions in outside cloud deals.

The Microsoft logo on the side of one of its office buildings.

Microsoft is winding down a 2023 deal to rent AI computing from Oracle after declining to renew it, Bloomberg reports. The servers ran older Nvidia chips. Microsoft is reassessing more than $80B in contracts with outside cloud providers.

Microsoft is winding down a deal to rent AI computing power from Oracle, Bloomberg reported on Friday, citing people familiar with the matter. Microsoft has been switching off the rented capacity in recent months after deciding not to renew the contract.

The servers ran older Nvidia chips and no longer matched what Microsoft needed, the people said. Ageing hardware is a growing worry: Gartner has warned that much AI infrastructure spending could face large write-downs as newer chips arrive.

The deal, announced in 2023, gave Microsoft Oracle servers to run its AI-powered Bing search, and Bloomberg could not determine its full size. Separate talks for Microsoft to lease more Oracle capacity collapsed in June, Business Insider reported.

Microsoft declined to comment. Oracle said demand for its AI infrastructure remains strong, including for older chips, and its shares fell about 3%.

Microsoft has committed more than $80B to rent capacity from other cloud firms such as CoreWeave, Nscale and Nebius. Its leaders have grown frustrated with their costs and delays, Bloomberg said. This week, it reportedly turned down an option to expand a Nscale deal in Texas, weeks after pulling out of a Nscale site in the UK.

The news adds to Oracle’s troubles. Heavy spending on AI data centres has pushed its free cash flow negative and its credit rating to one notch above junk. Its shares have more than halved since a June peak.

AWS’ Colleen Aubrey: Stop inventing new AI metrics — it’s your business metrics that matter

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

GUEST COLUMN by Zeus Kerravala

There isn’t a business or information technology leader today who doesn’t have artificial intelligence at the top of their priority list. The challenge for most organizations is deciding what to measure and how to determine whether an AI initiative was successful.

To better understand how customers should approach AI metrics, I spoke with Colleen Aubrey from Amazon Web Services Inc,, who has some deep experience in this area.

As senior vice president of Applied AI Solutions at AWS and a member of Amazon’s S-team, she builds on that foundation with lessons from 20-plus years at Amazon, where she built customer-facing products for millions of sellers and advertisers. The playbook she drew from that experience gives enterprises a practical path to move out of AI pilot purgatory, and it starts with throwing out the AI return-on-investment slide.

She argues that understanding AI ROI requires thinking in terms of business metrics. “I worry that people start trying to come up with new metrics to measure the impact of AI, but it’s the same metrics,” she explained. “It’s your business metrics that matter.” AWS has spent 20 years building cloud infrastructure. AI business applications are a much newer opportunity.

When Aubrey joined AWS, she inherited 35 application-layer programs. “I would say Connect for the contact center was probably our most successful example,” she told me in a recent interview ahead of AWS re:Invent Nov. 30-Dec. 4.

She studied what made it work: The team had deep operational experience running Amazon’s customer service function; it chose a sharp wedge (outstanding voice quality on the AWS backbone rather than chasing feature parity; and the team knew its customers. The lessons from that success are now the blueprint for the expanded Amazon Connect family: Connect Customer, Connect Talent, Connect Health and Connect Decisions.

The playbook Aubrey built for the Connect family closely mirrors what IT leaders need to move their own AI programs out of pilot mode. Here are my five takeaways that should matter to IT leaders:

The first lesson Aubrey drew from her assessment was about where to start. Nobody runs a better supply chain than Amazon, which is exactly why the early version of AWS Supply Chain was such a useful learning experience. When Aubrey got close to the product, she saw a team working to catch up rather than lead. They had been building five modules that “largely were about getting to feature parity” with entrenched enterprise resource planning vendors that had a decades-long head start.

Connect had taken the opposite path, and it’s instructive. “They understood they weren’t going to achieve feature parity,” Aubrey said of the Connect team. Instead, they led with outstanding voice quality on the AWS backbone, started narrow and built from there.

Aubrey applied that same lesson to the supply chain. She rebooted it as Connect Decisions by asking a different question: where is the pain most acute, and where is the current tooling weakest? The answer was planners. “They’re firefighting the whole time,” she said. “They’re overwhelmed by a whole bunch of signals. By the time they get to decide, the moment has passed.”

The same applies to enterprises. AI projects that stall often try to replicate an entire workflow. The ones that succeed identify a painful decision point and make it dramatically better.

Aubrey’s point about measurement deserves unpacking. “Companies are stuck on what’s the ROI of AI, and they’re looking at return on tokens and this sort of stuff,” she said. “The question is, can you move your business metrics by working in a different way?”

Her proof point is Wells Vehicle Electronics, which, according to AWS, cut inventory by 7% in three months while expanding the SKUs they actively monitor from 20 to 2,000. Nobody at Wells is talking about tokens. They’re talking about working capital. If a project can’t be tied to a metric the chief financial officer already tracks, it’s an experiment, not an investment.

Amazon started where most companies start: with software development, making AI tools available across the company. Aubrey admits there’s a bit of chaos at first. “Everyone’s just experimenting with a bunch of things,” Aubrey said.

She sees that as a feature, not a bug, because that’s where the real learning happens. “You go through this learning period of, you know what, our test coverage sucks,” she said. Fix test coverage, and you find “our pipelines suck,” so you fix pipeline health, eliminate manual intervention and deploy faster. The result in her group this year: a twofold improvement in the velocity of pushes to production.

The lesson is that the velocity didn’t come from the coding assistant. It came from fixing everything downstream that the assistant exposed. Enterprises that buy the tool and skip the plumbing will see activity rise while output stays flat.

I asked Aubrey whether Amazon has adjusted its goals to reflect what employees can now do with AI. Her answer was an honest “yes and no.” In her user experience team, roles have collapsed: designers are being pushed to ship code to an alpha environment, and Amazon has created a new “UX tech” role.

The “no” is that org design and goal-setting are still catching up. Her message to her team is blunt: “The only choice you can’t make is to own the task. You have to choose to own the outcome.” Every IT leader should borrow that line. Task-based job descriptions will age poorly.

AWS’s design philosophy for the Connect family is what she calls “humorphism,” the idea that AI should fit the way people already work, not the other way around. Her biggest concern is the lag between internal prototypes and production.

“Shipping good AI with a bad user experience will fail, in my opinion,” she said. Her team has invested heavily in closing that gap, moving beyond traditional command-based interfaces toward AI that picks up cues, remembers decisions, and carries work forward. The goal, she said, is to make AI so usable that people change how they work, not because they’re told to, but because it makes sense.

She was equally candid about maturity. TE Connectivity has scaled Connect Decisions to 2,000 planners, but Connect Talent reached general availability only in September and has early customers in deployment. Aubrey said the team is focused on getting the product right before aggressively scaling, reflecting the same “pick a wedge” discipline she applied across the Connect solutions.

What made this conversation valuable was Aubrey’s willingness to share how she thinks about building AI products that work in production, from studying what made Connect successful to applying those lessons across supply chain, hiring, health care, and customer engagement. That candor is a useful model for IT leaders, because most enterprise AI programs are stuck for the same reasons AWS’s applications once were: too much breadth, too little focus and success measured by the wrong yardstick.

The AI era won’t reward companies with the most pilots. It will reward those that pick a sharp problem, fix the plumbing it exposes, redesign work around outcomes and move the metrics they already have.

Zeus Kerravala is a principal analyst at ZK Research, a division of Kerravala Consulting. He wrote this article for SiliconANGLE.

Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

Rocco’s Black Friday Sale: Smart Fridges Hit Their Lowest Prices of 2026

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If you've been coveting one of Rocco’s smart designer minifridges, you might be interested to know the brand's biggest sale of 2026 starts today, featuring the brand-new Rocco Essential for under $1,000 and the OG Classic in this year’s new Beige colorway, as well as white and two rare blue colors, for $1,495—as low as the Classic ever gets.

Today also marks the rerelease of the Classic's Icy Blue and Royal Blue, two rarely seen colors that Rocco says are not coming back after this year.

The Essential was just released in August and just began shipping in September; I'm currently testing it now. It's only been about a week and a half, but early impressions are positive. It has the same smart sight system as the Classic, but the biggest difference is obviously the look. Vintage-inspired fluted glass and rounded edges are out; sleek minimalism is in. The tinted dark glass door takes up the entire front, with a color-matched metal handle you can swap out for midcentury-inspired oak for an extra $95.

It's only got one temperature zone as opposed to the Classic's two, but my favorite part so far has been the interior—instead of the Classic's old-fashioned wire racks, the Essential sports smooth-glide drawers lined with a rubbery material and grip strips. This makes it much more accommodating to oddly shaped drinks and prevents cans (it can hold up to 77) from rolling around every time you open your Rocco. It also gets colder than the Classic model—down to 34 degrees Fahrenheit instead of 37. My main frustration so far is that the sight system has been a little spotty and slow to recognize when drinks have been removed, but I'm having that problem with the Classic as well, so it's not specific to this model. All colors (Cream, Sage, Graphite) of the Essential are currently discounted.

The handsome, vintage-inspired model is the one that started it all. Its thick steel body comes in a range of fun colors, the fluted glass refracts potentially drink-damaging light, and it comes with a color-matched metal tray so it can double as a bar cart. It's got two isolated temperature zones for red and white wine (or extra-crispy Cokes), plus five full-sized racks and one half-rack—enough for 88 cans or 27 bottles of wine. The wire racks don't pull out as smoothly as the Essential's rubber-lined drawers, but this is otherwise a top-of-the-line beverage cooler with an impressive 10-year warranty.

You can also control the interior lights from your phone. The sight system, however, is buggy—my Classic is currently displaying a rack of Dr. Pepper and Stella Artois bottles I had left over from a party in July (they were consumed not long after that), so I would not recommend buying one of these for the sight system alone. (Note this sale is on the Beige, White, Royal Blue, and Icy Blue Classic only.)

I’m partial to the Classic’s forest green, but even I've got to admit the blue hues look pretty good. They sold out almost immediately around this time last year, so if you've been wanting one and you see this, I wouldn't wait to add it to your cart.
