Andy Warhol’s ‘Camouflage’ Portraits Inspired Piaget’s New Limited-Edition Watch

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Andy Warhol amassed quite the watch collection during his life—including a piece or two from Piaget (around seven, to be exact). And now, the maison has collaborated with the Andy Warhol Foundation for Visual Arts to bring an homage to the artist to your wrist.

Meet the Camouflage watch, a creation inspired by Warhol’s Camouflage self-portraits, which the artist crafted in 1986. As such, just 86 pieces of this iteration of the Andy Warhol watch will be made. Piaget takes the bold colors splashed across the American creative’s face in the series and plops them right on the dial. Here, sodalite and turquoise stones are the stars in waves of blue, paired alongside satin-finished gold and polished rose gold to add texture and dimension to that camouflage look. Piaget uses 27 pieces in all to make the dial, which it took over 100 hours of R&D and craftsmanship to come to life, into a blend of blue and warm hues. Related Articles Gagosian to Sell Works from Mr. Chow's Art Collection, Including Paintings by Warhol, Basquiat, Hockney, and More Miami Art Dealer Sentenced to Nearly Four Years in Prison for Selling Fake Warhols

Beating away inside the gadrooned white-gold case lies Piaget’s 501 P1 caliber, an automatic movement that powers those dauphine hands and offers up a 40-hour power reserve. Flip the watch over, and you’ll catch a glimpse of Warhol’s engraved signature, as well as the number of watches in the series.

“Our objective is to take inspiration from Andy Warhol’s creations without strictly reproducing each of his works on a dial,” Stéphanie Sivrière, artistic director of Piaget, said in a press statement. “The direct connection between Andy Warhol and Maison Piaget is color, particularly through the use of ornamental stones, which remains the common thread of this very distinctive timepiece.”

This isn’t the first time the duo has teamed up, either. In 2024, Piaget was given the go-ahead by the Andy Warhol Foundation for the Visual Arts to rename its Black Tie watch (itself a reinvention of the iconic Ref. 15102, a 45 mm cushion-shaped timepiece Warhol owned) in Warhol’s honor. Under the new license, the maison was also able to craft one watch annually that pulled from the artist’s oeuvre. As a result, Piaget dropped the limited Collage timepiece back in 2025, home to blocks of pink opal, green chrysoprase, and yellow serpentine on the dial, now followed by the 2026 iteration (price available upon request). We’ll just have to wait and see what’s in the cards for 2027, then.

As part of Art Week NYC, a citywide gathering celebrating contemporary art, Jean-Bernard Forot, Piaget’s Head of Patrimony, is speaking with ARTnews senior editor Maximilíano Durón about Warhol’s love of timepieces and the watchmaker’s collaboration with the Andy Warhol Foundation for the Visual Arts that grew out of it. The talk takes place at the PMC Studio on Saturday, November 14. RSVP here.

Meike Mixes It Up On APS-C With New $449 58mm f/1.2 Portrait Prime

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Meike Mixes It Up On APS-C With New $449 58mm f/1.2 Portrait Prime

Meike has announced yet another addition to its Mix series of primes, and this time it’s a portrait prime for APS-C mirrorless cameras. The Meike 58mm f/1.2 Mix is a fast $449 prime for Sony, Fujifilm, and Nikon mirrorless systems.

The Meike 58mm f/1.2 Mix includes 14 elements arranged across 10 groups and an 11-bladed aperture diaphragm. Meike promises “soft, smooth bokeh” and “fine detail.” While it’s impossible to say for sure whether the image quality is good with this new lens, the Mix series has proven effective so far in PetaPixel‘s hands-on testing.

With the approximately 1.5x crop factor on APS-C mirrorless cameras, the 58mm prime delivers an angle of view more like an 87mm (or roughly 85mm) prime lens on full-frame cameras. This is a very popular focal length for portraits, and one Meike already caters to on full-frame cameras with its 85mm f/1.4 Mix II AF STM.

Meike doesn’t specify what type of autofocus system its new portrait prime uses, but it promises fast autofocus that works alongside in-camera detection modes like eye-detect and face-detect AF. The lens focuses as close as 0.62 meters (just over two feet).

The Meike Mix series typically uses a straightforward, unassuming design, and this one is no different. However, while the black lens looks pretty standard, the lens also comes in more striking silver, white, and white-and-black colorways.

The lens is 90 millimeters (3.5 inches) long and has a 62mm filter thread. The lens weighs about 450 grams (15.9 ounces) on E and X mount, but the Nikon Z lens is a bit heavier at 455 grams (just over a pound). This is likely because the Nikon Z mount has a wider diameter. In any event, they’re all compact and lightweight.

The Meike AF 58mm f/1.2 Mix actually faces quite a bit of competition.

Sigma makes a 56mm f/1.4 DC DN Contemporary lens for APS-C Sony, Fujifilm, and Nikon cameras, plus other ones like Canon RF and L-Mount. It’s $454 right now thanks to a $125 holiday savings discount. While not quite as fast as Meike’s new lens, it offers a similar focal length and is much lighter at just 280 grams (9.9 ounces). It’s also a lot shorter, measuring just 59.5 millimeters (2.3 inches) long.

The Viltrox AF 56mm f/1.2 Pro is as fast as Meike’s new lens, but it’s over 100 grams heavier and ever-so-slightly longer. It’s available for $522 thanks to a 10% holiday discount on Sony E, Fujifilm X, and Nikon Z. This is probably the closest competitor to Meike.

For much less money, Viltrox also offers the AF 56mm f/1.7 lens. It’s slower, of course, but compact, lightweight, and just $180. It’s also on the same three mounts: E, X, and Z.

For Meike’s part, its new lens is $449, putting it just below the discounted Sigma 56mm f/1.4 and a fair bit less than Viltrox’s speedy f/1.2 prime. The Meike 58mm f/1.2 Mix is available now through Meike. While it’s not yet listed at B&H at the time of writing, it should be available there soon.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Cognizant announced on November 6, 2026 that it had acquired Wizeline, a global services firm focused on AI-native engineering and digital product development for enterprises. Financial terms were not disclosed.

The acquisition expands Cognizant’s work in digital product development, software engineering, cloud modernization, data and AI. Cognizant expects the added capabilities to strengthen its ability to help clients design, build, modernize and scale digital products, platforms and AI-enabled experiences.

Cognizant places the deal within its broader business of building full-stack AI solutions intended to connect AI investment with enterprise value. Wizeline contributes an AI-centric product-engineering model that pairs global forward-deployment teams with data and AI expertise for modern product and workflow projects.

Wizeline’s company profile reports approximately 2,000 employees, more than 100 clients and a presence in 11 countries. Its location directory separately lists operations in Canada, the United States, Mexico, Colombia, Argentina, the United Kingdom, Spain, Romania and Vietnam, spanning the Americas, Europe and Asia-Pacific. Wizeline operates as a nearshore transformation engineering partner for media and entertainment, financial services, and healthcare and life sciences enterprises.

Wizeline was acquired from its previous investors, including La Caisse. Its current leadership already includes direct Cognizant experience: Chief Executive Officer Inés Casares previously held leadership positions at Cognizant, Cognizant Softvision and Globant. She also served as Wizeline’s chief service officer, leading cross-functional teams across solutions, pre-sales, partnerships and talent acquisition.

Wizeline brings a concentrated media and entertainment franchise supporting large U.S. enterprises across publishing and news, filmed entertainment, broadcasting, and talent and creator representation. On its media-services page, Wizeline says four of the top six U.S. media conglomerates trust its services.

One agentic-AI accelerator coordinates agents for multimodal, intent-based search across asset libraries, archives, vector databases and metadata taxonomies through a single interface, without requiring all assets and metadata to be migrated into one place. Another uses multi-agent workflows to turn a master asset into platform-specific content, while a separate offering automates ingestion, metadata tagging, transcription, captioning, quality control, language checks and rights distribution.

Together, those accelerators cover distinct stages of media work: locating material, adapting it for different channels and preparing it for distribution. The acquisition therefore gives Cognizant a concrete route from its full-stack AI positioning into day-to-day content operations, backed by Wizeline’s engineering teams and client franchise. The central execution question is how broadly those specialized capabilities can be deployed across Cognizant’s enterprise client work.

Free Chinese AI Models Are Getting Scary Good at Hacking

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Open-weight artificial intelligence models are now almost as capable of hacking into computer networks as the best closed ones, according to new research that heralds a much-anticipated turning point in the cybersecurity landscape.

Vals, a company that measures AI capabilities, set out to test how well different AI models could carry out complex, multi-step network instructions on mock corporate networks.

“The best frontier models, locked up inside the labs and available only through trusted access programs, are now starting to be matched by open models that anybody can download,” says Nikil Ravi, a researcher at Vals.

The company’s findings suggest that closed models no longer have a definitive edge in cyber skills, meaning recent efforts by frontier labs to limit access to their models, and thereby slow the proliferation of such capabilities, may potentially be moot.

The findings are also noteworthy given that the US frontier labs, particularly Anthropic, premised the limited release of new models—beginning with Claude Mythos Preview in April—on the idea that gated access would only buy time before advanced hacking capabilities were broadly available.

Anthropic's frontier red team lead, Logan Graham, told WIRED in April that the company’s Project Glasswing, which gives trusted access to the full capabilities of new models, was focused on getting those tools “in the hands of defenders to give a head start.”

“We need to prepare now for a world where these capabilities are broadly available in 6, 12, 24 months. Many things would be different about security,” he told WIRED at the time.

Seven months later, that transition seems to have arrived on schedule.

Vals tested AI models in two simulated scenarios, giving them various specific tasks to achieve. The first scenario involved a mock corporate network accessible through a public-facing website. The second involved two different company networks; a model needed to compromise the first in order to gain access to the second.

The researchers found OpenAI’s most advanced cyber model, Daybreak, which is available only to trusted partners, to be the most capable, completing 81 percent of the tasks it was given in an average of two tests. The Chinese open-weight model DeepSeek was not far behind, completing 73 percent of the objectives on average. The Vals researchers note that on some tasks, the two models scored equally.

The research also showed how much cheaper it is to use an open-weight model for this kind of task. Vals says it costs $17 to $46 in tokens for GPT-6-Sol to carry out an attack compared to $2 to $3 for DeepSeek. Open models are generally cheaper because they can be run on one’s own hardware. Rayan Krishnan, CEO of Vals, says this also provides a strong incentive for those playing defense to use open models rather than proprietary ones.

OpenAI did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

Much of the year in cybersecurity has played out as predicted, with AI automating core hacking tasks to help both attackers and defenders find a massive number of new vulnerabilities in code and rapidly probe networks for weaknesses. While the speed and scale of the findings, and the intensity of fixing uncovered software flaws, have all increased, experts are still debating whether AI has fundamentally altered the conceptual foundation and tenets of cybersecurity defense. In other words, it’s still not clear whether AI tools pose a new kind of threat or amplify the security challenges that have long existed.

The fact that open-weight models are now starting to match closed ones is crucial for understanding how criminal hackers can harness AI, and how companies and developers should respond. But in practice, real-world observation of cybercriminal and state-backed hacking activity has repeatedly shown in recent months that attackers are doing what they always do by conserving their resources and generally making do with older and less capable models—both proprietary and open—for most of their activity. And even with strict controls, defenders must assume that the best-funded and most highly motivated attackers have already had at least some access to closed models all this time.

“I am in general agreement that open-weight models are capable of producing advanced attacks and vulnerabilities today, especially when you consider the abliterated models that disable safety training by modifying weights,” says Alex Zenla, cofounder and chief technology officer of the cloud security firm Edera. “My caveat to this is that it is likely that the closed labs have trained additional, unreleased models exceeding these capabilities. So I would put this very much like a cat-and-mouse game. The labs see themselves as moving fast to outpace the open-weight models, to be able to secure vulnerabilities before the open-weight models find them. Unfortunately, I do think this may be the beginning of a dangerous security landscape.”

French court orders restitution of Pissarro painting sold at Sotheby’s for £1.2m to heirs of Jewish banker

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Camille Pissarro's Darse de pêche et bassin Duquesne (fishing dock and Duquesne basin, 1902)

A French court has ordered the cancellation of the Sotheby’s London sale of a landscape by Camille Pissarro, and its immediate restitution to the heirs of Jewish banker Salomon Flavian, on the grounds that it was looted in Paris in 1941. According to the 5 November ruling, Sotheby’s has two months to reveal the “complete identity” of the buyer, subject to a daily penalty of €1,000 for any delay.

Entitled Darse de pêche et bassin Duquesne (fishing dock and Duquesne basin, 1902), this view of the port of Dieppe in Normandy, was sold at Sotheby’s London on 25 June 2024 for £1.2m.

The Sotheby’s catalogue did mention it had been owned by Flavian from 1935 and was registered in the Répertoire des biens spoliés (repertory of looted art) released by the French government in 1947. Nevertheless, it indicated that the work was “offered for sale pursuant to a settlement between the current owner and heirs of Salomon Flavian”. Their consent, Sotheby’s explained in court, had been obtained by the Mondex Corporation, a Toronto-based company specialised in the return of looted art.

At a court hearing on 18 September, Corinne Hershkovitch, the lawyer for seven heirs of Flavian, said this agreement had been signed by only two relatives and the others were not consulted. In court, Mondex and Sotheby’s endorsed a study which concluded that the painting had been acquired in the late 1930s by the British artist and collector Edward Le Bas, from the dealer Paul Rosenberg, and therefore was no longer part of Flavian’s collection seized in 1941 by the Nazis.

The court judged otherwise, considering that there was “no direct and convincing evidence” of these alleged transactions. On the contrary, the Flavian family’s research produced “a set of precise, serious and concurring elements demonstrating that Pissarro’s work was indeed looted during” the Second World War, according to the judges.

In a statement, a spokesperson for Sotheby’s London said it is “disappointed by the judgement regarding the restitution”. However, the auction house “welcomes the decision to dismiss all claims against Sotheby’s”. “The history of the artwork is complex”, the spokesperson adds, and “we fully recognise the profound losses suffered by the Flavian family during the Nazi era and, from the outset, have approached this matter responsibly and with the care and respect it deserves.”

“It is a very important ruling, which opens the way to a restitution that the Flavian family has demanded since the war,” Hershkovitch tells The Art Newspaper. “It shows Mondex’s failure in its mission, and now Sotheby’s has to bear the responsibility for having sold a looted artwork.”

Mondex’s lawyer, Melina Wolman, says the company intends to appeal a judgement that, she says, is “posing major difficulties in regard with historical factors”. She adds that the name of Le Bas, who owned the Pissarro painting in the 1950s, appears on an entry in the catalogue raisonné edited by Paul Rosenberg in 1939. But the court ruled that this was only one among “contradictory elements” and that Sotheby’s and Mondex “did not support this hypothesis by any direct proof of such sales, at a time when France was at peace and the art market well structured”.

In court, Remi Sermier, a lawyer for Sotheby’s, also disputed the court’s jurisdiction on a sale made in London. But the court stated that, under French law, the annulment of the 1941 dispossession acts makes all subsequent sales of the work null and void. However, the court chose not to cancel the agreement signed between Mondex and the two heirs who had waived all their rights to the painting just before the sale, in exchange for a share of the sale price.

The ruling could trigger a cascade of ligitation internationally. Sotheby’s is “considering whether to join the appeal”, a spokesperson says, stressing that “client confidentiality is of paramount importance” for the company and it is not ready to willingly disclose the identity of the painting’s buyer. The spokesperson adds: “There is a procedure that will need to be followed in order to enforce the decision in the UK.”

In case of success, the Flavian family will have to face the current possessor, who can demand compensation from Sotheby’s. For its part, the auction house could turn against Mondex and the consignor of the work, because, according to sources close to the case, the settlement agreement Mondex and the seller signed contains an indemnity clause of which Sotheby’s is the beneficiary.

Mass Effect-like RPG The Expanse: Osiris Reborn ropes in David Gaider, the original lead writer of Bioware's Dragon Age series

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

"Focusing on certain aspects of the game's narrative."

The Expanse: Osiris Reborn looks like it's carrying the best bits of Mass Effect forward, and in an encouraging move, the developers have been collaborating with David Gaider.

Of course, David Gaider is best known as the lead writer on Dragon Age: Origins, Dragon Age 2 and Dragon Age: Inquisition - he's also often credited as the architect of its dark fantasy world Thedas. Gaider's post-BioWare career saw him open his own game development house Summerfall Studios, which most recently released an ambitious roleplaying musical called Stray Gods.

But he's now lending a hand to a game that's not too dissimilar to BioWare's other hugely famous roleplaying series, while still working on his own games at Summerfall.

"Alongside my work at Summerfall, I've also been collaborating with the Owlcat Games team on The Expanse: Osiris Reborn, focusing on certain aspects of the game's narrative. The Expanse: Osiris Reborn shouldn't be confused for the other very Mass Effect-y RPG coming out next year, Exodus. Cheers At The Gate As American Airlines Pilots Save My Flight From Cancellation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Never have I been so happy to board a delayed American Airlines flight…because we actually took off when I thought we were certain to face cancellation. And it culminated in a way that left me smiling.

American Airlines is famous for its rolling delays and I recently encountered one on a flight from Chicago to Los Angeles. While AA has promised that it going to do a better job of communicating delays and trying to predict actual departure time based on aircraft placement, it still has some work to do.

My flight was originally supposed to leave at 7:10p but I received a text message in the afternoon that it would be delayed until 8:15p, which actually was idea for me. I showed up to the airport at 7:30p, had a quick bite to eat in the Admirals Club, and prepared to board…but boarding never began. Instead, the delay was pushed to 8:45p. Then it was pushed to 9:00p. Then 9:30p. Then 10:15p. Then 11:30p. Then 11:55p.

The other AA evening flight to Los Angeles was facing similar delays.

Why? The aircraft was on the ground and at the gate. It had been there since 7:45p.

One of the agents in the Admirals Club turned her screen to me and said, “See look, you’ve got no pilots.”

Sure enough, no pilots were assigned to my flight, though it made no sense to me…this was an A321 and were in Chicago O’Hare, a hub. Does AA have no reserve pilots there that can fly an Airbus?

During this time I went back and forth between the gate and the lounge, because the rolling delays notifications often came late and while I know you usually get a text message when boarding starts, that is not always the case and I did not want to miss the flight (like Andrea Sachs).

The poor gate agents were fielding the angry mob…people were livid and many were freaking out that the flight would not cancel. The gate agent didn’t exactly help by stating his belief the flight would cancel, but I appreciated his candor.

Back in the Admirals Club, suddenly the pilot positions were filled. They were being flown in from Dallas, said the agent.

Seems like a product of poor planning, but okay. I was offered a hotel room if I wanted to bail and rebook for tomorrow (“But it aint gonna be at the Hilton”) but I really wanted to get to LA without a short night in a hotel room.

Back at the gate, we were told that though the pilots had been assigned, it would be up to them determine whether they wanted to accept the overtime assignment and fly the flight…the gate agent once again was on the pessimistic side. Many passengers were (understandably) irate.

But around 11:15p, the Dallas flight pulled up to the gate. We all waited anxiously.

Suddenly, a first officer rounded the corner and the whole gate area broke out into cheers. Someone called out, “Are you our pilot?!” and he flashed the thumbs up sign.

Moments later, the captain showed up to another chorus of cheers.

The trip was on! Boarding soon commend and we were on our way…very late, but on our way.

And as an added bonus, since so many passengers bailed, I got a first class upgrade at the gate, minutes before boarding. Better than three seats in economy class? Probably not, but certainly better than just an open middle in Economy Extra and I ended up enjoying a delicious salmon dinner enroute to LAX and still enjoying a nap for a couple hours due to the late hour.

I wish AA would get their act together, but after hours of rolling delays, I was just thankful to get home without having to overnight in ORD.

PyTorch 2.15 tackles the 'one bad GPU' problem

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Technical debt shoveled aside to make large-scale AI training more resilient

ML admins will be happy to learn that one bad GPU may no longer mean starting an entire training job from scratch. The latest 2.15 release of PyTorch includes a rewritten backend for Nvidia's Collective Communications Library (NCCL), designed to improve fault diagnosis and recovery when GPUs work together.

This latest feature shows how its creators continue to advance the software from being a tool for researchers to being a cornerstone in production AI work.

The interface to the networking library in PyTorch needed a rewrite – it was drowning in technical debt, said PyTorch core developer Tristan Rice, a Meta software engineer specializing in distributed computing, during a live YouTube livestream on Thursday.

NCCL is pronounced "Nickel" and stands for the Nvidia Collective Communications Library. It provides PyTorch with a set of inter-GPU communication primitives, allowing GPUs to communicate within a single-node or across multi-node clusters, which is vital for running inference on large neural networks and for other tasks in building and maintaining LLMs.

The previous PyTorch backend for NCCL, called ProcessGroupNCCL, could spawn dozens of communicators per process group, leading to intermittent deadlocks and unpredictable performance.

The new version of the NCCL backend comes with a strict rule: One process group gets only one communicator during initialization. By default, this rule eliminates crosstalk between multiple communicators and all the complexities that entails. If a program requires parallelism, it will have to dispatch more process groups.

The updated library also better handles stragglers by allowing for easier reconfiguration. If a GPU crashes or runs slowly, a third-party orchestrator can exclude the laggard and even assign a replacement worker.

A “flight recorder” has also been added to give admins a better idea of what went wrong. Previously, if a cluster of GPUs stalled—perhaps from a timeout or deadlock—the user would just get a generic message. The new flight recorder captures all operations in a series of snapshots that can be inspected after a failure occurs.

Rice noted the cleaned-up NCCL backend, also called ProcessGroupNCCL, will give developers a blank slate, making it easier to add new features.

The legacy ProcessGroupNCCL is part of the 2.15 package as a rollback, but if the new NCCL backend works as expected, the older version will be removed in the 2.16 release, planned for early next year.

Why, you may ask, does a machine learning framework need a specialized communications library? Like NumPy, PyTorch supports numerical computing, but it also provides tools for building and training neural networks on CPUs and GPUs.

In these environments, workers must constantly exchange data such as tensors, gradients, activations, and weights. The standard TCP-based networking stack can introduce bottlenecks in large GPU clusters, so NCCL uses optimized communication paths to move data between GPUs while reducing CPU overhead.

Nvidia initially released NCCL at SC15 as a general-purpose accelerator, and it was adopted by the Facebook folks when they first built PyTorch in 2016. Their goal was to build a Python-based version of the Lua-based Torch ML library.

“Every major ML lab (OpenAI, DeepMind, Meta, Nvidia) uses PyTorch. It powers everything from diffusion models and LLMs to recommender systems and robotics. This widespread adoption isn’t accidental,” noted Will Stewart, co-founder of AI software provider Northflank, in a blog post.

Other new features of PyTorch 2.15 include a new Python transport API for asynchronous one-way data movement – ideal for training jobs where weights are synchronized between trainers and generators – as well as parallel processing improvements for Apple gear and for supporting Nvidia’s next-generation Blackwell GPUs.

Overall, the update consisted of 3,117 commits from 583 contributors. ®

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.

Twelve crime movies where justice actually prevails

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s an incredible week. Progressive democratic candidates SWEPT the elections. In the biggest congressional win of the last five decades, we flipped a bunch of red states, and elected smart, community-and-inclusion-minded leaders who prioritize healthcare, the environment, minority rights, and livable conditions. Abdul El-Sayed! Jon Ossoff! James Talarico! Gina Hinojosa! Annie Andrews! Melat Kiros! Amy Acton! Keisha Lance Bottoms of Georgia became the first Black woman governor in the whole country!!! It’s an incredible moment in American history, a true victory over the narcissistic, bigoted, murderous regime in power currently.

It’s been a great week, so let’s keep the party going. If you’re looking for something that will get you fired up for justice, this weekend, may I recommend one of the following twelve classic movies.

These are political/legal/journalism movies about moments that Truth and Goodness and Fairness prevail. And you know what? Many of them are based on a true story!

This is not a comprehensive list, just the movies I thought of in my post-election euphoria. Happy watching… and power to the people!

Frank Capra’s Mr. Smith Goes to Washington is about RAMPANT GOVERNMENT CORRUPTION so yes it’s a crime movie, and yes it’s very topical. Next slide, please.

Ah yes, the classic story about how two Washington Post reporters exposed the Watergate scandal and brought down Richard Nixon. O, for a president with Richard Nixon’s sense of decency. And that’s saying something.

Every single person in Congress should watch this movie, this weekend.

The whole point of America is for everyone—ANYONE—to have a fair trial.

Sure, the truth can be adjusted… but it will also eventually come out!

Sarah Polley’s adaptation of Miriam Toews’s somber, charged novel about women in a Mennonite community who collectively gather in the hayloft of a farm to address the systemic sexual abuse perpetrated upon them by the men of the colony (and to vote on what they should do about it), is riveting. It is a true ensemble film and feels often like a play—a brave and compelling story about women who decide not to accept what they are told they must.

The fight is worth it!!!!! Fight for the future!!!!!

If you haven’t, you should really watch this engrossing heist-ish thriller adaptation of Andreas Malm’s groundbreaking nonfiction book. Malm’s book argues that that peaceful protests have proven themselves ineffective in stopping the widespread annihilation of the earth and its inhabitants by climate change, and the only thing that can make any real change is property damage. The film follows a group of young people (mostly early 2os but a couple people around 30) as they band together with a specific mission: destroying a new oil pipeline that has been built in Texas. They all have different reasons for coming to this decision, but their effort will be both a symbol of resistance and a real, practical hindrance to the environmental ravaging posed by the pipeline.

This is a good movie, that constructively, caringly reenacts Jodi Kantor and Megan Twohey’s investigation into the crimes of Harvey Weinstein.

Remember when The Washington Post published the Pentagon Papers? A great moment for justice, a great moment for truth in America.

The TRUE story of the legal researcher who made a corporation pay for its toxic environmental destruction and public health dangers.

Miami Beach Dealer Faces Another Lawsuit from Artist Alleging Non-Payment

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Gabriel Kilongo, the dealer behind the Miami Beach–based gallery Jupiter, is facing another lawsuit from a third artist who alleges that he is withholding money from the sales of her art.

Yirui Jia, a young painter who had a solo show at Jupiter’s Miami Beach location in 2025, sued Kilongo in September in the Supreme Court of the State of New York, alleging that he had only paid her $2,000 of a $42,000 sum owed for the sales of five paintings.

It is one of four lawsuits filed in the past two years against Kilongo, whose gallery also formerly operated in New York. Others, as first reported by Urgent Matter, have centered around allegations of nonpayment, damage to art, and withheld artworks. The lawsuit regarding the damaged artwork appears to have been settled, while the others appear to still be pending. Related Articles Carsten Höller's Spiraling Silver Slides Appear in Mexico City as a Permanent Installation Rome's Galleria Borghese's $11.15 M. Extension Is Moving Forward Despite Pushback

According to Jia’s lawsuit, she received written assurances from Kilongo that he would pay her. He told her that he was “managing a very tight cash flow,” per an exchange quoted in the lawsuit, and stated that he was “waiting for a few things to clear on the gallery’s end” in order to remit payment. But he did not do this, the suit alleges, and therefore defied the consignment agreement they had signed.

The suit includes a sales report produced by Jupiter that notes that all five paintings had sold for values between $2,000 and $32,000. Two went to a Vancouver-based buyer named Bing Zhang, the document states, while the other three went to Bradley Chubb, the Buffalo Bills linebacker who was traded from the Miami Dolphins earlier this year.

“The complaint speaks for itself,” said a lawyer for Jia in a statement to ARTnews. Kilongo has not responded to the complaint at this time of writing.

He did not respond to ARTnews’s request for comment on Jia’s lawsuit specifically. But, responding to the four lawsuits facing the gallery, he said, “These are distinct proceedings, involving different circumstances and procedural histories, and should not be presented as a single narrative of wrongdoing.”

The dealer founded Jupiter in 2022, having previously worked at New York’s Mitchell-Innes & Nash gallery as an associate. The gallery has regularly shown at notable art fairs, including Untitled, Expo Chicago, and Zona Maco, and has even continued doing so as Jupiter has faced lawsuits, most recently appearing in the Independent New York art fair this past May.

Kilongo has previously touted his relationship with Jia, whose work was the subject of a solo show at Mitchell-Innes & Nash in 2024, the second to last exhibition there before the gallery transitioned to an advisory. “We have successfully placed works by artists—like Yirui, for instance—in prominent collections, including those of some museum trustees,” Kilongo said in an interview for Artnet’s Gallery Network program. “It’s always incredibly gratifying to see how her work holds up along other historical artists while still maintaining Yirui’s distinct and recognizable identity.”

Urgent Matter reported in May that Kilongo surrendered his New York gallery to his landlord and agreed to pay $25,000. It is unclear, however, whether Jupiter continues to operate in Miami Beach. The gallery’s website states that the most recently staged exhibition, a two-person show for George Clinton and Gerasimos Floratos, closed in Miami Beach this past February.

In Artnet News, Kenny Schachter reported that Jupiter was administratively dissolved on September 25, exactly one day after Jia’s lawsuit. Kilongo told ARTnews that this was not true and “does not accurately reflect [the gallery’s] current standing.”

Schachter’s column also stated that Kilongo, who was born in Democratic Republic of Congo and was raised in Israel, was detained by ICE and has since been released. Schachter attributed confirmation to James Snyder, director of New York’s Jewish Museum, but ARTnews could not establish a clear connection between Kilongo and that institution beyond the dealer having served on a host committee for a young collectors event held at the museum this past October. A database on ICE’s website that allows users to track detainees does not contain an accessible record for Kilongo.

Schachter did not mention in his column from this week that he had himself exhibited with Jupiter in 2025. (In a prior column from earlier this year, Schachter mentioned the Jupiter exhibition, which he described as “a generally positive experience.” “I sold the majority of the works after the exhibition (which Gabriel encouraged), but struggled to get paid for the few the gallery did manage to place, even those stemming from my existing relationships,” Schachter wrote.) Others to have exhibited with Jupiter include Emmanuel Louisnord Desir, Baseera Khan, Marcus Leslie Singleton, and Travis Fish.

The first of the lawsuits against Kilongo’s business was filed in 2025 by Jade Thacker, who alleged that she was owed $204,000 because he failed to pay her out for one sale and return works shown in 2024 that did not sell. Kilongo has not responded to Thacker’s lawsuit.

That lawsuit was followed by one in February 2026 from Location Incorporated, a New York entity based in the East Village that claimed Kilongo’s gallery had inflicted $1.6 million in damage to a sculpture shown at the Untitled Art, Miami Beach fair in 2024. Though the lawsuit did not identify the artist or work, Jupiter brought a work that year by New York–based artist Izhar Patkin. That lawsuit was dismissed and later settled, according to Urgent Matter.

And in March 2026, artist Stephanie Pierce sued Kilongo, echoing Thacker’s allegations by claiming that she was owed money for works sold in 2023 and that he withheld artworks. She sought $100,000 in damages. In this case, Kilongo responded by denying the allegations. Serving as his own legal representative, he said that “payment relating to the works has been made and that the return of the remaining works is actively in progress, with a majority already returned.”

New York program funds $13 million in energy storage advancement projects

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

New York State’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program has awarded nearly $13 million to 15 projects to support product development and demonstration of scalable long duration and other advanced battery energy storage solutions.

“Investing in next-generation energy storage technologies is critical to building a flexible and resilient grid that can make the most of our diverse energy resources,” said Doreen M. Harris, NYSERDA president and CEO. “These projects will advance promising solutions that can deliver power when it is needed most, strengthen grid performance and help drive down costs as we build a more affordable and reliable energy system for New York consumers.”

NYSERDA’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program awards projects that develop and demonstrate innovative long duration energy storage (LDES) and other under-utilized, alternatives to traditional lithium-ion technologies. The awarded technologies include new designs for scalable LDES solutions that are over 10 hours in duration at rated power and will address cost, performance, and integration challenges such as grid congestion, hosting capacity constraints and lithium-ion siting limitations.

Public Service Commission Chair Rory Christian said, “Storage is poised to play an increasingly critical role in supporting a reliable, cost-effective, flexible, and lower emissions system as more clean energy resources are integrated into the grid, as well as enhance the efficiency of the state’s existing resources. NYSERDA’s dedication of these funds originating from innovation and research funding overseen by the Commission positions New York’s consumers to yield the promising long-term benefits expected by more complex and scalable applications of these technologies.”

All contracts are awarded to companies located in New York, partnered with New York companies or for development and demonstrations in the state. Today’s announcement supports NYSERDA’s Innovation and Research (I&R) program, which invests over $96 million per year to attract world-class energy innovators, reduce risk for private investors and remove barriers to clean energy adoption in New York State, leveraging $15 in additional investment for every $1 spent. NYSERDA has partnered with over 900 companies that have helped make more than 300 products commercially available for consumers, businesses and utilities.

Power Generation and Storage Innovation funding for comes from the state’s Clean Energy Fund.

<i><em>MobLand</em></i> Season 2, Episode 8 Takes a Devastating Turn

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Conrad’s grip on reality is beginning to slip.

Last week I wrote a giddy hagiography of Frankie Cooper. It’s embarrassing how much I like him. I really should grow up, be a man, maintain some critical distance, and write in wry asides like a real English journalist.

But I can’t help it. He’s such an irresistibly murderous bastard that he makes me want to kill a cat just to see how it feels. (The lawyers have advised me to clarify that I don’t actually want to kill a cat; no cats were harmed in the making of this opinion.)

Well, Frankie, it’s been lovely. But there’s a new psychopath in town. Of course the Medusa had a vengeful son by the name of “Mad Dog”! Just out of prison for murder, he’s seen CCTV footage of Eddie, who was there when his daddy was killed, and he’s coming to the Cotswolds.

“I’ll find them, Daddy,” says Kelly, pouring a bottle of whisky over his father’s coffin.

But all that’s for episode 9. We’ve got the admin of episode 8 to sort through first.

Remember Harry’s words at the end of episode 7? “Is that Michael Cooper? At 3 a.m. you’re gonna get clapped.”

Well, Harry did indeed have Frankie Cooper’s boy killed. But not quite so easily as we were first led to believe. He wanted to fuck with Frankie in the process. Harry knew that Michael would call his dad, who has Newgate Prison sewn up tighter than a dodgy tailor’s pocket. Frankie therefore arranged for Michael to be moved to the solitary wing, where he would be safe.

But as Harry explains to Kevin: “What Frankie’s forgot is that a bent screw is a bent screw… So if the CM’s [custodial manager] gonna take a tenner, what’s he gonna do for a twenty? Or in this case, 200 grand in a black bag in the dead of night on a pissing-down Thursday.”

Harry then pays a couple of hired goons to string Michael up in his cell. Frankie is not going to be happy.

It’s time we gave Emmett J. Scanlan his flowers . He’s been ticking over in the background, doing a solid, likable job as the Harrigan dogsbody Paul. But he is changing now and emerging like a butterfly into the show’s comic sideline. There’s a real touch of the Baldricks about him, with a stroke of Hamlet’s gravedigger—the loyal dogsbody who gets lumbered with the shitty jobs and stays funny in spite of it all.

The poor sod thinks he’s in for the noose after the Sicily debacle of episode 7. He’s begging for his life, so desperate that he thinks VIP tickets to Rod Stewart might save him. But no. They were never going to kill Paul. It was merely a simple lampoon. They want him to cut up Donnie’s stinking body in a shipping container.

So Paul strips down to his leopard-print pants and, swinging his hips to the Rolling Stones’ “Mr Charm,” sets about chopping Donnie into vacuum-packable “bricks.” It’s all very Tom Cruise in Risky Business, but with more blood and less hairbrush. Long live Paul.

Eddie’s sinking deeper into the soup—and it’s all of his own doing. If only he weren’t—in Kevin’s words—such a “lazy, spoilt, entitled little cunt.” Maeve finds him in the sauna. He tells her their “situation” isn’t going to work.

“Maeve, I fear our relationship is sailing into choppy water. You’re 80. I’m barely legal.”

Maeve feigns surprise. “This approach, this ploy. It’s depraved. And deeply optimistic.”

Conrad, Eddie explains, is going to “piss his kegs.” Suddenly, so is Eddie, but not out of laughter. “Please don’t tell him; I’ll do anything.”

She agrees, but only if Eddie apologizes. On his knees. To her phone camera. It’s all fun and games for Maeve. I suspect we haven’t seen the last of that video.

Jan agrees to meet Harry. She wants to come clean, to pour out her heart. She tries to explain that she had a brief affair with a man who was using her to get to Harry and Conrad. But Harry already knows.

“That’s my job,” he tells her. “To anticipate danger. Control outcomes. Minimize unwanted consequences.”

She asks him about killing. About the nightmares. He says he doesn’t have any. You get the feeling Harry is all out of nightmares. Then he offers to help Jan and Gina run. Forever.

“I can make that happen. Tonight,” he tells her. “You never have to see me again.”

“What about you?” she asks. “Does no part of you want that?”

For a moment, Harry almost seems human. Almost.

It’s clear now: Conrad is losing his marbles. He wanders into the woods, calling out for his dead dogs. Then he stops, surrounded by swirling mists and squawking crows, and seems to realize just how far he’s slipping. He summons Kevin to the Cotswolds, to which the latter reluctantly submits. Conrad tells his son he’s seen a doctor. He wants to talk succession plans. Kevin, astonished at being asked such a question, reels furiously through the options.

First, Eddie: “A lazy, entitled psychopath who I wouldn’t trust to light the barbecue.”

Then Seraphina: “She would make a world-class businesswoman, but a kingpin? I don’t think she’s ever even touched a gun.”

And then, just as Kevin is about to pitch himself, Conrad reveals what’s really going on. “I see the dead,” he hisses with a haunted, hounded look, the air of the ancient mariner. “They come to me at night. I see them in the woods. All the bodies, in the clothes they died in, their wounds fresh. Standing there, staring at me. They’re in my head, they’re in my soul.”

It’s all very King Lear. They embrace and it’s lovely.

Kevin goes to get the old man a brandy to calm his nerves. When he returns, the fear is gone from Conrad’s eyes. In its place: pure steel. And a shotgun pointed at Kevin’s head.

“If you breathe a word of this to Bella, I’ll kill you both,” he growls.

Conrad is another man again. As though the whole confession were just a performance. But it wasn’t. It was a glimpse of the terrifying disintegration of a man who can no longer distinguish between his different selves.

Kevin can’t believe it. “Pull the trigger then,” he shouts snottily.

“Fuck off now, Kevin,” Conrad says. And, like a beaten dog (though of course, Conrad would never treat a dog in this way), off Kevin fucks.

Well, we’ve spent most of this episode wondering about Frankie’s response to the murder of his son. But here we are. Harry calls Frankie. He wants to reach a treaty. He’s killed Frankie’s son, and now he hopes Frankie will back down.

Here we see a side to Frankie we haven’t seen before: crying, blubbering, a human man in real pain. He’s looking through old photographs of Michael.

“He’s dead, Harry,” he says through snot and tears. “Why did you have to do that? I can’t explain it. The pain.”

“It’s the game,” says Harry, heavy as stone.

To which Frankie replies: “Anyway, I’m sure you understand. After all, Harry, you’re a father too. Ain’tcha?”

It’s a less-than-veiled threat; Frankie’s message: this war isn’t over until the babies are all dead. He’s going after Gina.

And so we end the episode in truly miserable style. A montage of sadness, soundtracked by Johnny Cash’s cover of “Ain’t No Grave”:

The Harrigans have made a lot of enemies over the past eighteen episodes. And now, at last, the dead are joining the queue.

Teaser for Honda’s Future Japanese-Market Crossover May Hint at Designs for America

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The 0-Series EV line may never reach production, but the new logo and design language those cars inspired seem to be on the way soon.

When Honda abandoned its plans to release a series of 0-Series-branded electric vehicles earlier this year, it also effectively abandoned the first cars that would have been released in the company’s bold new design language. That certainly does not mean the new look of Honda is being tabled forever, though. We already know from a hybrid sedan concept shown in May that the visual identity of the 0-Series EVs would live on—and now, we have our first look at a production car that shares that fresh look.

Both Honda of Australia and Honda of Japan posted a teaser for a model called the Avancier on Friday, revealing a first look at an SUV expected to be targeted primarily at those markets. Notably, the model appears to bear a passing resemblance to both the scrapped 0-Series SUV and the May concept. “Honda Hybrid” badging also suggests that the Avancier will be one of the company’s first cars to use the brand’s next-generation gas-electric powertrain.

The Avancier badge has previously been used on a turn-of-the-millennium station wagon for Japan, as well as a Chinese-built crossover offered in that market. This new model seems to be a crossover targeted at other markets, something that could be equivalent to the American CR-V or Passport. A representative from American Honda has confirmed to Road & Track that the Avancier itself is not coming to the U.S. market.

Details of the final model, including specifics of the Honda Hybrid powertrain, may prove irrelevant to American buyers, but the styling and the concepts behind the hybrid system could preview what to expect when Honda unveils a fast-tracked new CR-V for the United States in the near future.

The Business Platinum Card® from American Express

As High As 300,000 points. Find Out Your Offer.

As High As 175,000 points. Find Out Your Offer.

I’ve held the (see rates and fees) for eight years, and despite carrying more than 25 credit cards, it’s one I’ll continue renewing year after year.

The reason is simple: World of Hyatt is my favorite hotel loyalty program, and this card makes it even more rewarding to stay with the brand. Between an annual free night, automatic elite status and the ability to earn valuable Hyatt points on spending, I consistently receive far more value than the card’s affordable $95 annual fee.

It isn’t the right fit for everyone. Hyatt has a much smaller global footprint than Marriott, Hilton and IHG, so travelers who rarely stay at Hyatt hotels may be better served by a more flexible travel rewards card. However, if Hyatt is already part of your travel plans — or you’d like it to become one — the World of Hyatt card is one of the best mid-tier hotel cards on the market. Card rating*: ⭐⭐⭐⭐

*Card rating is based on the opinion of TPG’s editors and is not influenced by the card issuer.

If you’re comparing several hotel credit cards, here’s what you need to know at a glance:

Unlike cards that earn transferable rewards, this is very much a Hyatt-focused product. If you’re loyal to Hyatt — or want an affordable way to accelerate your progress toward higher status — it deserves serious consideration.

If there’s one reason why I continue paying the World of Hyatt card’s annual fee, it’s the anniversary free night certificate.

Each cardholder anniversary, you’ll receive a free night award valid at any Category 1-4 Hyatt hotel or resort. Since Hyatt prices these properties between 3,000 and 25,000 points per night, there’s plenty of opportunity to extract well over $95 in value. Free nights expire 12 months after issuance.

I’ve redeemed my certificates at The Standard, Brussels, Hyatt House Manchester in the U.K. and Andaz Mexico City Condesa. In each case, paying cash would have cost considerably more than the card’s annual fee.

Even better, you can earn a second Category 1-4 certificate after spending $15,000 on the card in a calendar year.

That’s the spending threshold I personally target each year. It unlocks another free night and an additional six elite night credits toward Hyatt status for even more value beyond the points you'll earn from that spending.

Related: The best Hyatt Category 4 hotels to maximize your free night award certificates

Simply holding the card gives you complimentary Discoverist status.

While Discoverist isn’t Hyatt’s most rewarding elite tier, I still appreciate benefits like preferred room upgrades and late checkout (when available) and a 10% bonus on eligible Hyatt spending.

That said, for frequent Hyatt guests, this perk probably won’t be all that enticing since they’re likely to earn Discoverist status organically. The card becomes more valuable when working toward a higher status level.

Every calendar year, you’ll receive five qualifying night credits automatically, and you’ll earn another two qualifying night credits for every $5,000 spent.

I’ve found those elite night credits incredibly valuable. By combining the five annual elite nights from the card with my Hyatt stays and strategic spending to earn an additional six nights, I qualified for top-tier Globalist status in August 2025, which I’ll enjoy through February 2027.

Related: Was going all-in on Hyatt Globalist worth it? My honest take

Chase issues this card, so you'll receive several useful travel protections, including:

These protections aren't as extensive as those you'd receive with a premium travel card, but that's to be expected given this card's modest annual fee.

Related: Is the World of Hyatt credit card worth the annual fee?

One of my favorite things about the World of Hyatt program is that earning and redeeming points is refreshingly straightforward.

Unlike most major hotel loyalty programs, Hyatt still publishes an award chart, making award pricing more predictable.

When you stay at Hyatt hotels, these earnings stack with the points you earn as a World of Hyatt member. Since cardholders receive complimentary Discoverist status, you’ll also earn a 10% bonus on your base points, meaning you can earn up to 9.5 total points per dollar spent on eligible Hyatt purchases. That represents a solid 16% return on Hyatt purchases, per our November 2026 valuations.

I generally use this card for Hyatt stays and tax payments that help me reach the $15,000 annual threshold for the second free night certificate.

Otherwise, I typically use my  (see rates and fees) for most travel and dining purchases, then transfer those Chase Ultimate Rewards points to my Hyatt account whenever I need to top up my balance for an award stay.

That gives me the best of both worlds: I earn more rewards on everyday travel and dining while keeping my points flexible until I’m ready to book. If I don’t end up needing Hyatt points, I can always transfer them to one of Chase’s 13 other airline or hotel partners instead.

Related: 6 of the best credit cards for travel purchases — beyond flights and hotels

In my opinion, Hyatt points remain among the most valuable hotel points due to their flexibility.

Award nights start at just 3,000 points for a Category 1 property at the Lowest rate and range up to 75,000 points for a top-tier Category 8 property under Hyatt’s five-tier standard award chart.

I’ve used Hyatt points for everything from inexpensive city breaks to luxury resorts. Some of my favorite redemptions include:

Hyatt also lets you redeem points for Points + Cash stays, suite upgrades, club lounge access and Find experiences. While those options can make sense occasionally, I almost always get the best value by redeeming points for standard award nights.

Related: 22 best Hyatt hotels in the world to book with points

Despite being one of my favorite hotel credit cards, the won’t be the right fit for every traveler.

The biggest drawback is Hyatt’s relatively small footprint. While Hyatt has more than 1,500 hotels and all-inclusive properties across 80-plus countries, that’s still far fewer than Marriott or Hilton. If you frequently travel to destinations without a Hyatt, you may struggle to maximize the card’s benefits.

Those who prioritize earning the highest possible rewards on everyday spending will likely find better options elsewhere. Beyond Hyatt purchases and spending toward the $15,000 threshold for a second free night certificate, this card isn’t particularly rewarding for dining, travel or everyday purchases.

Finally, travelers looking for premium perks like airport lounge access, annual travel credits and luxury benefits across multiple hotel brands should consider a premium travel rewards card instead.

For most travelers, I think the personal World of Hyatt Credit Card is the better choice than the (see rates and fees).

Both cards help you earn Hyatt points and elite status, but the business version is designed primarily for business owners. It offers bonus categories geared toward business spending and additional benefits for companies, while the personal card provides better long-term value for most travelers, thanks to its annual Category 1-4 free night certificate.

By comparison, I think the World of Hyatt Business card's $199 annual fee is harder to justify year after year because it lacks an anniversary free night. It does offer up to $100 in Hyatt statement credits each anniversary year, but that requires Hyatt spending and there's no way to get outsized value like you might with a free night.

Unless you own a business and can maximize the business-specific earning categories, I’d start with the personal card.

Related: World of Hyatt card vs. World of Hyatt Business card: 2 valuable hotel earners

The currently offers new cardholders four free nights (at Category 1-4 Hyatt hotels) after spending $5,000 on purchases in the first three months from account opening.

These are hotels that would ordinarily require 3,000 to 25,000 points per night, depending on the property and stay dates. Someone who uses all four free night certificates at the highest-priced properties would save up to 100,000 World of Hyatt points, making this offer worth up to $1,650 (based on TPG valuations).

This is a very strong bonus compared to other historical welcome offers, though not the absolute all-time highest offer for new cardmembers. The best-ever welcome offer included five free-night awards (offer no longer available). Having said that, if you max out the four certificates, this would be considered the most lucrative offer since February.

Despite being a great offer right now, some travelers may prefer to wait until the introductory bonus is awarded in points instead. Unlike free nights, there are no hotel category restrictions for redeeming points. The expiration date for free nights is also stricter.

Related: Your guide to the World of Hyatt award chart

I’ve carried the World of Hyatt Credit Card for eight years, and it’s one of the few hotel credit cards I have no intention of canceling.

The annual Category 1-4 free night certificate has consistently offset the card’s $95 annual fee, while the elite night credits helped me qualify for Globalist status last year. Add in Hyatt’s strong redemption value, and it’s easy to see why this remains my favorite mid-tier hotel credit card.

If you regularly stay at Hyatt properties, I think the World of Hyatt card deserves a place in your wallet. If you rarely stay with Hyatt, though, you’ll probably get more long-term value from a flexible travel rewards card.

Spurs have one Preston North End transfer clause that already looks doomed

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Preston North End's 12-season stay in the Championship is in serious jeopardy based on their early season form.

The Lilywhites were long-term stayers in the second tier the last time they were here, keeping themselves in from 2000 until 2011 with some near promotion misses along the way, before dropping into League One.

Since 2015, PNE have been back in the Championship, mostly being a mid-table team, albeit with some brief flirtations with the top six and also the drop zone, and there was hope over the summer that a switch in transfer business would unlock a new level for them.

North End are usually quite frugal with their transfer budget, cherry-picking some younh, high-quality Premier League loanees and opting to recruit permanent players on cheaper fees or free transfers.

There was much evidence of that last summer when Alfie Devine, Lewis Dobbin, Daniel Jebbison and Harrison Armstrong arrived on loan, whilst they saved paying fees until January of last season, when Odel Offiah, Andrew Moran and Callum Lang all arrived at Deepdale.

Football League World ranks all 24 Championship clubs by the longest-serving member of their squad.

For whatever reason though, that tact changed this past summer, with around £15 million spent on new faces and over £5 million recouped on Milutin Osmajic and Ben Whiteman's sales.

And £6 million of that windfall spent was making the loan signing of Devine a permanent one, which came as a surprise to many North End fans, given the interest surrounding his services.

Celtic, Southampton and Wolves were among the clubs that were keen on taking Devine from Spurs, but even after the initial deadline was missed to seal a deal with the playmaker, PNE were able to convince the 22-year-old that Deepdale was the best place for his future.

Devine was once the youngest player and scorer for Tottenham when he featured in an FA Cup clash with Marine in 2021, but he had to go out on several loans to develop, with stints taken in at Port Vale, Plymouth Argyle and Belgian outfit Westerlo before ending up at PNE.

And with Tottenham being a big-spending club looking to turn around their fortunes under Roberto De Zerbi, there was no room for Devine in his midfield for 2026-27, leading to the decision to let him move on permanently for a decent fee, much like they did with Will Lankshear and Ash Phillips when selling the pair to Middlesbrough.

Spurs hold extra clauses when it comes to Lankshear and Phillips, which means they have a vested interest in their futures and will want Boro in the Premier League, as well as matching rights on the pair, which means they are given the chance to match any accepted bid that comes in for their services - it's not quite like that with Devine, but there's still a sell-on clause in there.

The Daily Mail reported at the time of the deal that Tottenham had negotiated a 20 per cent sell-on clause with PNE for Devine, which is based on any profit the Lancashire outfit end up making on Devine - as it stands though, that could be in jeopardy.

The reason why Devine was so in-demand during the summer was his form for North End, with eight goals scored from midfield and six assists registered.

But he's showed nothing like that kind of ability for PNE in the 2026-27 season, scoring just one goal and notching one assist, with the Lilywhites looking like they're in big relegation trouble already.

Ahead of their 15th match of the Championship season against Southampton, North End have just five points on the board and are nine points from safety, which whilst not confirming relegation by any stretch of the imagination, they'll need to go some way to overturn the form they've showed so far.

Paul Heckingbottom's early sacking and club icon Graham Alexander coming into replace him hasn't helped results whatsoever, and Devine is yet to show what he's really capable of - with the general consensus of the fanbase being that he's missing the link-up with now-Southampton forward Dobbin that he shared last season.

Type the most common nickname next to each of the 24 clubs in the 2026–27 Championship. Leave off “The”. Some popular alternatives count too.

Clubs in the 2026–27 Sky Bet Championship, listed alphabetically.

Undoubtedly there's a lack of quality in the North End squad compared to last season, and that's showing on the pitch - Alexander didn't even turn to Devine in their 2-0 loss to Stoke City when he was sat on the bench, and he had to wait until the second half earlier this week against Sheffield United to come on, assisting Callum Lang's consolation goal in a 3-2 loss.

With the current trajectory that PNE find themselves going in, Devine will likely not be playing in League One next season if PNE find themselves there, but they won't be getting their £6 million back based on this season's performances, no matter how much potential the former Wigan Athletic youth prospect has.

It would also mean that Spurs do not get any more money for Devine despite their 20 per cent sell-on clause, which they will find a shame, as they would've been banking on Devine continuing to prosper at Deepdale - unlike their clauses for Lankshear and Phillips, Devine's clause is one that they can perhaps almost forget about.

Musk says government work will be under 10% of SpaceX revenue

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

AI computing, led by Anthropic, has overtaken NASA and the Pentagon as SpaceX's main source of money.

Elon Musk at VivaTech in Paris, 16 June 2023

Elon Musk said government contracts will make up less than 10% of SpaceX’s revenue this quarter, down from 16.6%. SpaceX now earns most from renting AI computing, with Anthropic its biggest customer, though Anthropic can exit from early 2027.

Government contracts will make up less than 10% of SpaceX’s revenue this quarter, Elon Musk said on X, Bloomberg reported on Friday. That is down from 16.6% in the third quarter and roughly a fifth in 2025.

Musk said the share would fall below 5% about a year from now, Reuters reported. NASA and the Pentagon were long SpaceX’s most important customers, paying it to launch astronauts and national security satellites.

The money now comes from AI. In the third quarter, SpaceX’s AI unit brought in $7.56B, more than Starlink, mostly by renting out computing power from its data centres in Memphis. Anthropic alone accounted for 34.5% of revenue, making it SpaceX’s biggest customer.

That brings its own risk. Anthropic can exit its contracts with 90 days’ notice from early 2027, and Wall Street has recently soured on companies that rent out AI computing, Bloomberg said. Musk himself warned last month that few will survive without other sources of revenue.

Musk’s comments came days after Democrats won control of both houses of Congress. He spent more than $445M backing Republicans, most of whom lost, and Bloomberg said that could strain his companies’ ties with the new Congress. SpaceX still holds a NASA contract to land astronauts on the Moon with Starship as early as 2028.

8 Best Anime Where Tiny Details Become Major Story Revelations

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Usama Mehmood is a Senior Writer at DualShockers with more than five years of experience in the video game industry. He has been writing professionally since 2021 and covering games since 2022, with work spanning guides, lists, reviews, and features across action-adventure games, JRPGs, open-world titles, racing games,

You know, I’ve rewatched certain anime scenes more times than I’d like to admit on YT or just browsing social media, and it's almost never for the big moments. It’s usually for something small: a character’s hand hovering a half-second too long or a line of dialogue that sounded like filler. Then, twenty episodes later, the show unexpectedly turns that one detail into the thing that rewrites almost everything you thought you knew about it.

Watched These Once? These 8 Anime Hit Harder the Second Time Around

It’s like that foreshadowing or missing puzzle piece coming back later that just makes you bewildered for a second or two at the show’s ingenious attention-to-detail, or, well, certain parts of it, to be exact. It made rewatches feel like detective work, and it also makes you appreciate how much patience and planning go into a story that pays off its smallest choices.

So this anime list is for the shows that reward that kind of attention, especially the ones where you can catch the smallest details and understand why they become so prevalent later, during the climax. Naturally, there are lots of spoilers ahead for the discussed anime, so watch out!

It’s funny that I recommended this show to a friend who went into Higurashi expecting a wholesome slice-of-life show, only to regret trusting my recommendation. It's a horror show that takes some characters’ quirks and uses them as pivotal anchors in later episodes.

Take the first arc, for instance: when Keiichi starts scratching his throat, you get the impression that it's a nervous habit. A few arcs later, you realize it’s the first symptom of the Hinamizawa Syndrome, which explains why people who were perfectly friendly suddenly turn on each other.

The twins are another example. See, you know Mion and Shion Sonozaki look nearly identical, and the show plays with that on purpose, but once you know how the identity swap works, you start to doubt every earlier scene where “Mion” seemed slightly off. I can get why some people think this show is straight-up torture, but don’t knock it till you try, am I right?

Let me preface this by saying we don’t argue between Aronofsky’s Black Swan and Satoshi Kon’s Perfect Blue; I love both. This cult-favorite anime movie’s best trick is how it plays with your sense of what’s real. Mima’s decision to quit her pop group CHAM! and become an actress feels like a simple career move, but as you watch, the film shows how every “step forward” she takes strips away a piece of her.

The tiny, uneasy moments, like her flinching at the fax of the Double Bind script or the way her reflection starts reacting a half-beat late, are the film silently telling you that Mima isn’t in control of her own image anymore. And that’s one of the key reasons I adore this movie, even after all these years since I first watched it.

These anime plot twists flipped entire stories on their heads.

On a first watch, Madoka Magica looks like a bright, pastel magical girl series… until you rewatch it and realize it was telling you exactly how it would break your heart from the very first episode. Almost everything in the show, from the unusual designs of the Witch's Labyrinths to the seemingly innocent conversations with Kyubey, carries far more meaning than it initially lets on to you.

I mean, for crying out loud, the show's opening, with its seemingly disconnected imagery and Madoka chasing after a mysterious figure, becomes much more meaningful once you understand what's really happening. Even small character choices carry weight. Mami’s tea parties, her loneliness, and her insistence on keeping up the cheerful senpai act are all easy to read as charm early on in this show.

Now I’ll be very real with you all here; Odd Taxi looks like a quiet slice-of-life show about a walrus cab driver named Hiroshi Odokawa, and it’s hard to say anything about it without spoiling it. He’s a dry, awkward man who talks in deadpan dad jokes and likes listening to a comedy podcast between fares.

Then a missing girl, a yakuza fixer, an idol group, a struggling comedian, and a nurse with a secret all begin orbiting his cab. What looks like a pile of unrelated conversations turns out to be a tightly built puzzle, and almost every piece of it was planted in the very first episodes.

Early on, much of the dialogue feels like filler, with passengers rambling about their lives or Odokawa making small talk, until you later realize this throwaway stuff sets up most of the plot because a casual remark about a missing person, an offhand comment about someone’s job, or even a name dropped once in passing can fill in the main structure of the mystery. The show trusts you to notice, and it rewards you.

Dive into anime with controversial endings with modern shockers like Death Note and classics such as Evangelion.

Everyone knew this was going to be here, and it deserves it even if it's an obvious pick for this list. Death Note is an anime practically built around the idea that the smallest details can make the biggest difference.

What starts as a straightforward cat-and-mouse game between Light and L quickly becomes an elaborate battle of deduction, manipulation, and psychological warfare, where a seemingly insignificant action can expose someone's entire plan.

Whether it's L noticing the peculiarities of Kira's early killings or Light carefully manipulating the Death Note’s rules, the show constantly rewards those who pay attention. It's incredibly satisfying to watch everything click into place, and while the latter half isn’t everyone’s favorite for obvious reasons, the finale is genuinely one of the most memorable endings in the entire anime medium.

I might be biased for including this because Steins;Gate is genuinely one of my favorite anime of all time, and just a few months ago in August, I had the chance to review the Steins;Gate Reboot VN—and the entire thing was just as good as I remembered.

It starts as a quirky science-fiction adventure about a group of eccentric friends experimenting with a microwave, then accidentally stumbles into a conspiracy-theory time-travel adventure that gradually becomes one of the most intricately woven narratives in the medium. I know that sounds like a mouthful, but anyone who’s seen the show will know the series is just like that in terms of writing.

The genius is in how casually the show introduces certain characters, conversations, messages, and seemingly throwaway moments before revealing just how important they are to the bigger picture at the very end, or just when a certain pocket watch strikes 7:35 PM. Sorry, had to do it.

Look, we’re not even going to get into the debate about how the show ended, so this will focus strictly on Attack on Titan’s brilliant foreshadowing, which led to major story revelations and more twists, as my listicle’s title already suggests.

No matter what you think of his directional choices for the last act, Isayama has an incredible knack for planting seemingly insignificant details throughout the story. Things like the truth behind the Walls, the mystery surrounding Eren's basement, and the peculiar behavior of certain Titans initially seem like separate mysteries, but eventually come together in ways that completely change your understanding of the world in later seasons.

Even ordinary moments in both the manga and anime involving characters like Reiner, Bertholdt, and Annie become far more interesting once you pay attention to their behavior or dialogue, since they ultimately foreshadow their twists.

Naoki Urasawa is one of the most legendary manga auteurs with a wide range of back-to-back bangers under his umbrella, but his work in Monster is honestly one of the best things you’ll ever get to see, as long as you’re patient enough.

This slow-burn psychological thriller is the kind of story where an innocent conversation or a character's seemingly insignificant memory can carry far more weight than you initially realize. Even some of the twists thrown in here at times can recontextualize things you've already witnessed in previous episodes, making them feel completely different when you look back at them.

The mystery surrounding Johan, the Kinderheim 511 experiments, and the disturbing picture-book stories are all excellent examples of how Urasawa plants little breadcrumbs throughout the narrative before gradually revealing their significance.

From One Piece's grand treasure to Itachi's tragic illness, these unsolved anime mysteries leave fans guessing.
